Defined terms Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Overview IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements sets out the principles for the presentation and preparation of consolidated financial statements when an entity controls one or more other entities. # Chapter 7 discusses the requirements of IFRS 10 relating to the concepts underlying control of an entity (a subsidiary) by its parent, the requirement to prepare consolidated financial statements and what subsidiaries are to be consolidated within a set of consolidated financial statements. It also covers the definition of an investment entity and the exception from consolidating particular subsidiaries of an investment entity. The development, objective, and scope of IFRS 10 are dealt with in Chapter 7 at sections 1.2 and 2. This chapter deals with the accounting requirements of IFRS 10 relating to the preparation of consolidated financial statements. In particular, it covers topics such as: consolidation procedures, when to commence or cease consolidation, how to account for the acquisition of a subsidiary that is not a business (business combinations are addressed in Chapter 10), how to account for a loss of control (including deemed disposals and distributions in kind), how to account for changes in ownership interest without a loss of control, and how to account for non-controlling interests (NCI). The chapter also covers the accounting for put and call options over NCI (‘NCI puts and calls’). Finally, the chapter includes a section on future developments being considered by the IASB or the IFRS IC. What you need to know • Consolidated financial statements represent the financial statements of a group (i.e., the parent and its subsidiaries) in which the assets, liabilities, equity, income, expenses and cash flows of the parent and its subsidiaries are presented as those of a single economic entity. • Investment entities do not consolidate particular subsidiaries, instead including them at fair value through profit or loss. IFRS 10 provides specific guidance on the definition of and accounting for investment entities. • If the subsidiary is not wholly-owned, the profit or loss and each component of other comprehensive income (OCI) of the subsidiary, and the equity of the subsidiary, are attributed to the parent’s interest and to NCI. This allocation is usually done on the basis of existing ownership interests. IFRS 3 Business Combinations addresses the initial measurement of NCI arising from a business combination. However, IFRS accounting standards do not address the initial measurement of NCI arising from an acquisition that is not a business and different approaches have developed. • A parent consolidates a subsidiary from the date on which the parent first obtains control, and ceases consolidating that subsidiary on the date on which the parent loses control. • On a loss of control of a subsidiary, the group deconsolidates the assets and liabilities and any NCI relating to a former subsidiary. Any retained interest in the former subsidiary is measured at fair value. A gain or loss is recognised in profit or loss equal to the fair values of the consideration received and retained interest less the carrying amounts of the assets and liabilities and any NCI in the subsidiary that is deconsolidated. Certain elements of OCI relating to the former subsidiary are required to be reclassified to profit or loss on a loss of control. • Where a subsidiary is sold (or contributed) to an associate or joint venture accounted using the equity method, conflicts between IFRS 10 (which requires a full gain or loss to be recognised in profit or loss on loss of control of the subsidiary) and IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures (which requires a partial gain or loss only to be recognised in a ‘downstream transaction’) have led to diversity in accounting approaches. The IASB and IFRS IC have also discussed other situations involving the loss of control of a subsidiary where it was unclear whether the requirements of IFRS 10 and/or other IFRS accounting standards applied. • Increases or decreases in the ownership interest in a subsidiary that do not involve a loss of control are accounted for in equity. There is no change to the assets (including goodwill) and liabilities recognised in the consolidated financial statements but there may be reallocations in equity between the parent’s interest and NCI in the subsidiary. • IFRS 10 does not directly address the accounting for NCI puts and calls. IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation requires that a financial liability is recognised for the present value of the redemption price for an NCI put. There is diversity of accounting approaches in accounting for NCI puts and this issue has been addressed in the IASB’s Financial Instruments with the Characteristics of Equity Project. An Exposure Draft Financial Instruments with the Characteristics of Equity was published in November 2023. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Introduction This chapter deals with the accounting requirements of IFRS 10 relating to the presentation and preparation of consolidated financial statements. IFRS 10’s requirements on consolidation procedures are dealt with in section 2 below; accounting for changes in control in section 3 below; accounting for changes in ownership interest without loss of control in section 4 below; the recognition, measurement and presentation of NCI in section 5 below; and accounting for NCI puts and calls (or combinations of such options) in section 6 below. The final section covers future developments in section 7 below. FASB differences Differences between the IFRS and US GAAP guidance on this topic are not addressed in this chapter as the standards are not converged. Therefore, differences between IFRS and US GAAP may exist that are not addressed in this publication. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Consolidation procedures 2.1 Basic principles Extract from IFRS 10 Appendix A Defined terms consolidated financial statements The financial statements of a group in which the assets, liabilities, equity, income, expenses and cash flows of the parent and its subsidiaries are presented as those of a single economic entity. [IFRS 10 Appendix A]. The above approach to preparing consolidated financial statements is referred to as ‘the entity concept’. As noted in Chapter 7 at section 10, an investment entity generally measures its investments in subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 Financial Instruments, with limited exceptions. **[IFRS 10.31, IFRS 10.32, IFRS 10.33].** IFRS 10 requires a parent to prepare consolidated financial statements using uniform accounting policies for like transactions and other events in similar circumstances (see section 2.6 below). [IFRS 10.19, IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B87]. Consolidation of an investee begins from the date the investor obtains control of the investee and ceases when the investor loses control of the investee. [IFRS 10.20, IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B88]. In order to present financial information about the group as that of a single economic entity, the entity must: [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B86] (a) Combine like items of assets, liabilities, equity, income, expenses, and cash flows of the parent with those of its subsidiaries (b) Offset (eliminate) the carrying amount of the parent’s investment in each subsidiary and the parent’s portion of equity of each subsidiary (IFRS 3 explains how to account for any related goodwill - see # Chapter 10 at section 13) [IFRS 3.B63(a)] (c) Eliminate in full intragroup assets and liabilities, equity, income, expenses, and cash flows relating to transactions between entities of the group (profits or losses resulting from intragroup transactions that are recognised in assets, such as inventory and fixed assets, are eliminated in full). Intragroup losses may indicate an impairment that requires recognition in the consolidated financial statements. IAS 12 Income Taxes applies to temporary differences that arise from the elimination of profits and losses resulting from intragroup transactions. See section 2.4 below. Income and expenses of a subsidiary are based on the carrying amounts of the assets and liabilities recognised in the consolidated financial statements at the acquisition date. Many items will have a fair value on acquisition that will affect subsequent recognition of income and expense. IFRS 10 gives the example of depreciation expense, which will be based on the fair values of the related depreciable assets recognised in the consolidated financial statements at the acquisition date. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B88]. Point (b) above refers to the elimination of the parent’s investment and the parent’s portion of equity. The equity in a subsidiary not attributable, directly or indirectly, to the parent, represents an NCI. [IFRS 10 Appendix A]. The profit or loss and each component of OCI of a subsidiary are attributed to the owners of the parent and to the NCI. [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B94]. NCI in subsidiaries (in aggregate) are presented within equity, separately from the equity of the owners of the parent. [IFRS 10.22]. Changes in a parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary that do not result in the parent losing control of the subsidiary are accounted for as equity transactions. [IFRS 10.23]. Accounting for NCI is discussed in more detail in sections 2.2, 4 and 5 below. 2.2 Proportion of subsidiary’s profit or loss, OCI and equity allocated to parent and NCI The basic consolidation procedures described above effectively mean that 100% of the assets, liabilities, income, expenses, and cash flows of a subsidiary are consolidated with those of the parent, irrespective of the parent’s ownership interest in the subsidiary. However, the profit or loss and each component of OCI of the subsidiary, and the equity of the subsidiary, are attributed to the parent and the NCI (if the subsidiary is not wholly-owned). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests As discussed in Chapter 7 at section 4.3.4, when assessing control, an investor considers any potential voting rights that it holds as well as those held by others. Common examples of potential voting rights include options, forward contracts, and conversion features of a convertible instrument. If there are potential voting rights, or other derivatives containing potential voting rights, IFRS 10 contains the following guidance on the proportion of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity allocated to the parent and NCI (see section 5.7 below) in preparing consolidated financial statements: [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B89, **IFRS 10.B90, IFRS 10.B91]** Extract from IFRS 10 Potential voting rights B89 When potential voting rights, or other derivatives containing potential voting rights, exist, the proportion of profit or loss and changes in equity allocated to the parent and non-controlling interests in preparing consolidated financial statements is determined solely on the basis of existing ownership interests and does not reflect the possible exercise or conversion of potential voting rights and other derivatives, unless paragraph B90 applies. B90 In some circumstances an entity has, in substance, an existing ownership interest as a result of a transaction that currently gives the entity access to the returns associated with an ownership interest. In such circumstances, the proportion allocated to the parent and non-controlling interests in preparing consolidated financial statements is determined by taking into account the eventual exercise of those potential voting rights and other derivatives that currently give the entity access to the returns. B91 IFRS 9 does not apply to interests in subsidiaries that are consolidated. When instruments containing potential voting rights in substance currently give access to the returns associated with an ownership interest in a subsidiary, the instruments are not subject to the requirements of IFRS 9. In all other cases, instruments containing potential voting rights in a subsidiary are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9. Generally, the proportion of NCI is determined solely on the basis of existing ownership interests. It does not reflect the possible exercise or conversion of potential voting rights and other derivatives. [IFRS 10.21, **IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B89, IFRS 10.B94].** Usually, there is no difference between the existing ownership interests and the present legal ownership interests in the underlying shares. However, allocating the proportions of profit or loss, OCI, and changes in equity based on present legal ownership interests is not always appropriate. For example, there may be situations where the terms and conditions of the potential voting rights mean that the existing ownership interest does not correspond to the legal ownership of the shares. IFRS 10 recognises that, in some circumstances, an entity has, in substance, an existing ownership interest as a result of a transaction that currently gives it access to the returns associated with an ownership interest. In such circumstances, the proportion allocated to the parent and NCI is determined by taking into account the eventual exercise of those potential voting rights and other derivatives that currently give the entity access to the returns. **[IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B90].** Where this is the case, such instruments are not within the scope of IFRS 9 (since IFRS 9 does not apply to subsidiaries that are consolidated). [IFRS 9.2.1(a)]. This scope exclusion prevents double counting of the changes in the fair value of such a derivative under IFRS 9, and of the effective interest created by the derivative in the underlying investment. In all other cases, instruments containing potential voting rights in a subsidiary are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B91]. Illustration 2-1 below shows this principle. Illustration 2-1: Proportion of subsidiary’s profit or loss, OCI and equity allocated to parent and NCI where potential voting rights exist Entities A and B hold 40% and 60%, respectively, of the equity of Entity C. A also holds a currently exercisable option over one third of B’s holding (of shares in C) which, if exercised, would give A a 60% interest in C. The terms of the option are such that it leads to the conclusion that C is controlled by and therefore is a subsidiary of A, but do not give A present access to the returns of the underlying shares. Therefore, in preparing its consolidated financial statements, A attributes 60% of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity of C to the NCI. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests How we see it Whether potential voting rights and other derivatives, in substance, already provide existing ownership interests in a subsidiary that currently give an entity access to the returns associated with that ownership interest will be a matter of judgement. Issues raised by NCI puts and calls, including whether or not such options give an entity present access to returns associated with an ownership interest (generally in connection with a business combination) are discussed further in section 6 below. That guidance may also be helpful in assessing whether potential rights and other derivatives, in substance, give present access to returns associated with an ownership interest in other contexts. This chapter uses the term ‘present ownership interest’ to include existing legal ownership interests together with potential voting rights and other derivatives that, in substance, already provide existing ownership interests in a subsidiary because they currently provide the entity access to the returns associated with an ownership interest (as described in IFRS 10.B90). [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B90]. 2.2.1 Attribution when NCI change in an accounting period NCI may change during the accounting period. For example, a parent may purchase shares in a subsidiary held by NCI. By acquiring some (or all) of the NCI, the parent will be allocated a greater proportion of the profits or losses of the subsidiary in periods after the additional interest is acquired. [IFRS 10.BCZ175]. Therefore, the profit or loss and OCI of the subsidiary for the part of the reporting period prior to the transaction are attributed to the owners of the parent and the NCI based on their ownership interests prior to the transaction. Following the transaction, the profit or loss and OCI of the subsidiary are attributed to the owners of the parent and the NCI based on their new ownership interests after the transaction. 2.3 Consolidating foreign operations IFRS 10 does not specifically address how to consolidate subsidiaries that are foreign operations. As explained in IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates, an entity is permitted to present its financial statements in any currency (or currencies). If the presentation currency differs from the entity’s functional currency, it needs to translate its results and financial position into the presentation currency. Therefore, when a group contains individual entities with different functional currencies, the results and financial position of each entity are translated into the presentation currency of the consolidated financial statements. [IAS 21.38]. The requirements of IAS 21 in respect of this translation process are explained in Chapter 16 at section 6. A reporting entity comprising a group with intermediate holding companies is permitted to adopt either the ‘direct method’ or the ‘step-by-step method’ of consolidation. IFRIC 16 Hedges of a Net Investment in a Foreign Operation refers to these methods as follows: • Direct method - The financial statements of the foreign operation are translated directly into the functional currency of the ultimate parent. • Step-by-step method - The financial statements of the foreign operation are first translated into the functional currency of any intermediate parent(s) and then translated into the functional currency of the ultimate parent (or the presentation currency, if different). An entity has an accounting policy choice of which method to use, which it must apply consistently for all foreign operations. [IFRIC 16.17]. Both methods will result in the same amounts in the presentation currency for the statement of financial position. However, this does not necessarily hold true for income and expense items particularly if an indirectly held foreign operation is disposed of (as the different methods impact the amount of cumulative retranslation reserve subsequently reclassified to profit or loss). [IFRIC 16.BC36]. Differences will also arise between the two methods if an average rate is used, although these are likely to be insignificant. See Chapter 16 at sections 6.1.1, 6.1.4 and 6.6.3 for further discussion and a comparison of the effect of the two approaches on accounting for a disposal of an indirectly held foreign operation. IFRIC 16 also provides guidance on what does and does not constitute a valid hedge of a net investment in a foreign operation, and on how an entity determines the amounts to be reclassified from equity to profit or loss for both the hedging instrument and the hedged item, where the foreign operation is disposed of. It notes that in a disposal of a subsidiary by an intermediate parent, the use of the ‘step-by-step method’ of consolidation may result in the reclassification to profit or loss of a different amount from that used to determine hedge effectiveness. An entity can eliminate this difference by determining the amount relating to that foreign operation that would have arisen if the entity had used the ‘direct method’ of consolidation. However, IAS 21 does not require an entity to make this adjustment. Instead, it is an accounting policy choice that should be followed consistently for all net investments. [IFRIC 16.17]. IFRIC 16 is discussed in more detail in Chapter 16 at sections 6.1.4 and 6.6.3 and Chapter 49 at section 5.3. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 2.4 Intragroup eliminations IFRS 10 requires intragroup assets and liabilities, equity, income, expenses, and cash flows relating to transactions between entities of the group to be eliminated. Profits or losses resulting from intragroup transactions that are recognised in assets, such as inventory and fixed assets, are eliminated in full as shown in Illustration 2-2 in section 2.4.1 below. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B86(c)]. Even though losses on intragroup transactions are eliminated in full, they may still indicate an impairment that requires recognition in the consolidated financial statements. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B86(c)]. For example, if a parent sells a property to a subsidiary at fair value and this is lower than the carrying amount of the asset, the transfer may indicate that the property (or the cash-generating unit to which that property belongs) is impaired in the consolidated financial statements. This will not always be the case as the value- in-use of the asset (or cash-generating unit) may be sufficient to support the higher carrying value. Transfers between companies under common control involving non-monetary assets are discussed in # Chapter 9 at section 4.4.1; impairment is discussed in Chapter 21. Intragroup transactions may give rise to a current and/or deferred tax expense or benefit in the consolidated financial statements. IAS 12 applies to temporary differences that arise from the elimination of profits and losses resulting from intragroup transactions. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B86(c)]. These issues are discussed in Chapter 29 at sections 7.2.5 and 8.7. The application of IAS 12 to intragroup dividends and unpaid intragroup interest, royalties or management charges is discussed in Chapter 29 at sections 7.6.4, 7.6.5 and 8.5. Where an intragroup balance is denominated in a currency that differs to the functional currency of a transacting group entity, exchange differences will arise. See Chapter 16 at section 6.3 for discussion of the accounting for exchange differences on intragroup balances in consolidated financial statements. 2.4.1 Intragroup eliminations - allocation between parent and NCI IFRS 10 does not specify how the elimination of profits and losses are to be allocated between the parent and NCI. Illustration 2-2 shows how intragroup transactions are eliminated in both upstream and downstream transactions between a parent and subsidiary. Illustration 2-2: Eliminating intragroup transactions Entity A holds a 75% interest in its subsidiary, Entity B. A sold inventory costing CU100,000 to B for CU200,000, giving rise to a profit in A of CU100,000. B still held the inventory at the end of the reporting period. Tax effects are ignored in this example. Under IFRS 10, as well as the intragroup sale between A and B, the unrealised profit is eliminated from the group’s point of view in consolidation as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Revenue in A Cr Cost of sales in A Cr Inventory in B The profit from the sale of inventory of CU100,000 is reversed against group profit or loss. As the parent made the sale, no amount of the eliminated profit is attributed to the NCI. If the fact pattern was reversed, such that B sold inventory to A, and A still held the inventory at the end of the reporting period, the CU100,000 of profit would still be reversed in the consolidated financial statements. However, in this instance, as the subsidiary made the sale, CU25,000 of the eliminated profit (i.e., the NCI’s 25% share of the CU100,000 profit) would be allocated to the NCI. Therefore, the reduction in profit would be attributed to the owners of the parent and NCI, in their proportionate interests, 75% and 25%. If the inventory held by B had been sold to a third party for CU300,000 before the end of the reporting period (resulting in a profit in A of CU100,000 for the sale to B at CU200,000 and a profit in B of CU100,000 for the sale to a third party at CU300,000), no intragroup elimination of profit is required. The group has sold an asset with a cost of CU100,000 for CU300,000 creating a profit to the group of CU200,000. In this case, the intragroup elimination is limited to the sale between A and B as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Revenue in A Cr Cost of sales in B Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 2.5 Non-coterminous accounting periods The financial statements of the parent and its subsidiaries used in the preparation of the consolidated financial statements shall have the same reporting date. If the end of the reporting period of the parent is different from that of a subsidiary, the subsidiary must prepare, for consolidation purposes, additional financial information as of the same date as the financial statements of the parent, unless it is impracticable to do so. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B92]. ‘Impracticable’ presumably means when the entity cannot apply the requirement after making every reasonable effort to do so. [IAS 1.7]. If it is impracticable for the subsidiary to prepare such additional financial information, then the parent consolidates the financial information of the subsidiary using the most recent financial statements of the subsidiary. These must be adjusted for the effects of significant transactions or events that occur between the date of those financial statements and the date of the consolidated financial statements. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B93]. How we see it IFRS 10 does not give examples of ‘significant transactions or events’ that must be adjusted. However, there will commonly be items that will need to be eliminated as part of the consolidation process such as distributions from the subsidiary to the parent, cash transfers or settlement of intragroup balances. Care is needed to ensure such transactions are properly eliminated in order to avoid double counting effects, e.g., where assets transferred intragroup might otherwise be reported by both the subsidiary and parent. Significant transactions could also include significant acquisitions or disposals, impairments or developments on contingencies or provisions in the intervening period between the dates of the subsidiary and parent financial statements. The difference between the date of the subsidiary’s financial statements and that of the consolidated financial statements must not be more than three months. The length of the reporting periods and any difference between the dates of the financial statements must be the same from period to period. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B93]. It is not necessary, as in some national GAAPs, for the subsidiary’s reporting period to end before that of its parent. How we see it The requirements relating to coterminous accounting periods seem to imply that, where a subsidiary that was previously consolidated using non-coterminous financial statements is now consolidated using coterminous financial statements (i.e., the subsidiary changed the end of its reporting period), comparative information is restated so that financial information of the subsidiary is included in the consolidated financial statements for an equivalent period in each period presented. However, other approaches not involving restatement of comparatives might sometimes be acceptable (as discussed below). Other approaches not involving restatement of comparatives might be acceptable, particularly where the comparative information had already reflected the effects of significant transactions or events during the period between the date of the subsidiary’s financial statements and the date of the consolidated financial statements. Where comparatives are not restated, additional disclosures might be needed about the treatment adopted and the impact on the current period of including information for the subsidiary for a period different from that of the parent. IAS 21 addresses what exchange rate is used in translating the assets and liabilities of a foreign operation that is consolidated on the basis of financial statements made up to a different date to the reporting date used for the reporting entity’s financial statements. [IAS 21.46]. This issue is discussed further in Chapter 16 at section 6.4. 2.6 Consistent accounting policies IFRS 10 requires the use of consistent accounting policies by the group in consolidated financial statements. Accordingly, if a member of the group uses accounting policies that differ from those adopted in the consolidated financial statements for like transactions and events in similar circumstances, appropriate adjustments are made to that group member’s financial statements in preparing the consolidated financial statements to ensure conformity with the group’s accounting policies. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B87]. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Changes in control 3.1 Commencement and cessation of consolidation A parent consolidates a subsidiary from the date on which the parent first obtains control, and ceases consolidating that subsidiary on the date on which the parent loses control. [IFRS 10.20, IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B88]. IFRS 3 defines the acquisition date, which is the date on which the acquirer obtains control of the acquiree, [IFRS 3.8, IFRS 3 Appendix A], (see Chapter 10 at section 4.2). The requirement to continue consolidating (albeit in a modified form) also applies to a subsidiary held for sale accounted for under IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations (see Chapter 5). 3.1.1 Acquisition of a subsidiary that is not a business The same basic requirement to consolidate a subsidiary (see section 3.1 above) also applies when a parent acquires a controlling interest in an entity that is not a business, as defined in IFRS 3 (see Chapter 10 at section 3.2). [IFRS 3.3, IFRS 3 Appendix A, IFRS 3.B7-B12D]. As required by IFRS 10, the parent consolidates all investees that it controls, not just those that are businesses, and recognises any NCI in the subsidiary (see section 5 below). IFRS 3 states that when an entity acquires an asset or group of assets that is not a business, the acquirer allocates the cost of the group between the individual identifiable assets acquired and liabilities assumed based on their relative fair values at the date of purchase. Such a transaction or event does not give rise to goodwill. [IFRS 3.2(b)]. The cost of the group of assets is the sum of all consideration given, any NCI recognised, and transaction costs incurred, if any. The allocation of the transaction price (or cost) to the identifiable assets acquired and liabilities assumed is discussed further in Chapter 10 at section 2.2.2. However, neither IFRS 10 nor IFRS 3 address how NCI in the entity is initially measured, where the entity is not a business. How we see it In our view, if the NCI has a present ownership interest and is entitled to a proportionate share of net assets upon liquidation, the acquirer of a subsidiary that is not a business has a choice to recognise the NCI on initial measurement in one of the following ways: (a) Its proportionate share of the fair values of the entity’s identifiable net assets (b) Its fair value (measured in accordance with IFRS 13 Fair Value Measurement) (c) Its proportionate share of the consolidated book values of the net assets, including transaction costs. The basis for including transaction costs is that these are included in the consolidated book values of the subsidiary’s net assets Or (d) Its proportionate share of the consolidated book values of the net assets, excluding transaction costs. The basis for excluding transaction costs is that these are borne by the parent and are not part of the net assets attributable to the NCI In all other cases, NCI is recognised at fair value (measured in accordance with IFRS 13), unless another measurement basis is required in accordance with IFRS accounting standards (e.g., any share-based payment transaction classified as equity is measured in accordance with IFRS 2 Share-based Payment - referred to as a ‘market-based measure’ - see section 5.2 below. As discussed in section 2.2 above, IFRS 10 recognises that, in some circumstances, an entity has, in substance, an existing ownership interest as a result of a transaction that currently gives it access to the returns associated with an ownership interest. Consequently, there may be situations where no NCI is recognised in relation to, say, a derivative over a subsidiary’s shares where the parent has, in substance, an existing ownership interest over those shares. Approaches (a) and (b) above are consistent with the choices on the initial recognition of NCI required by IFRS 3. [IFRS 3.19]. However, it could be argued that an asset acquisition is an economically dissimilar transaction to a business combination, and therefore the guidance in IFRS 3 is not a ‘similar and related issue’ under the hierarchy in IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors for selection and application of accounting policies.1 [IAS 8.11(a)]. Under this view, Approaches (c) and (d) above, which are based on the proportionate share of the consolidated book values of the entity’s net assets, could also be supported. The consequential amendments resulting from IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial Statements, once effective, changes the title of IAS 8 to ‘Basis of Preparation of Financial Statements’ to better reflect the amended content of IAS 8. See # Chapter 4 for further details on the requirements of IFRS 18. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 3-1 below is an example of an acquisition of a subsidiary that is not a business (where there is no pre-existing interest). The determination of NCI where the entity acquired was an associate or joint venture accounted under the equity method is explained in Illustration 3-2 in section 3.1.2.B below. Illustration 3-1:2 Acquisition of a subsidiary that is not a business Entity A pays CU160,000 to acquire an 80% controlling interest in the equity shares of Entity B, which holds a single building (measured using the cost model under IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment) that is not a business. The fair value of the building is CU200,000. An unrelated third party holds the remaining 20% interest in the equity shares. The fair value of the NCI is CU40,000. In this case, the fair value of the NCI (as an equity interest) is the same as the NCI’s proportionate share of the fair values of the identifiable net assets of B. Transaction costs are CU4,000. Tax effects, if any, are ignored in this example. As noted in section 3.1.1 above, there are four approaches to determining NCI which are illustrated below. Approach (a) - measure NCI based on the proportionate share of the fair values of B’s identifiable net assets A initially records the following accounting entry: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Building Cr **NCI (20% × CU200,000)** Cr Cash Approach (b) - measure NCI at its fair value A initially records the following accounting entry: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Building Cr NCI (at fair value) Cr Cash Approach (c) - measure NCI based on the proportionate share of the consolidated book values of B’s net assets, including transaction costs A initially records the following accounting entry: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Building Cr NCI (at 20% ÷ 80% × CU164,000) Cr Cash The calculation effectively grosses up the consideration paid by the parent (together with transaction costs) and determines the NCI’s proportionate share. This approach has the effect that the NCI is recorded at its proportionate share (20%) of the consolidated book values of the net assets, including transaction costs. Approach (d) - measure NCI based on the proportionate share of the consolidated book values of B’s net assets, excluding transaction costs A initially records the following accounting entry: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Building Cr NCI (at 20% ÷ 80% × CU160,000) Cr Cash Illustrations which involve NCI assume that the NCI has a present ownership interest equal to the proportionate equity interest held, except where otherwise noted. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 3.1.2 Step acquisition of an associate or joint venture (equity accounted) that is not a business and becomes a subsidiary An entity that has an interest in an associate or joint venture that does not constitute a business may obtain control and so become a parent of a subsidiary that does not constitute a business. Current IFRS accounting standards do not address the accounting treatment for an acquisition in stages from associate or joint venture to a subsidiary that is considered as an asset acquisition rather than a business combination. This raises questions as to how to measure the previously held interest and the NCI on acquisition. 3.1.2.A Measuring the previously held interest As discussed in section 3.1.1 above, the cost of the group of assets is the sum of all the consideration given (including transaction costs incurred, if any) and any NCI recognised. It is therefore necessary to determine the ‘cost’ of the previously held interest, being part of the consideration given for the group of assets acquired. [IFRS 3.2(b)]. In January 2019, the IFRS IC issued an agenda decision regarding a different, but relevant, example of the step acquisition of an investment in a subsidiary accounted for at cost in the separate financial statements of the parent. The IFRS IC concluded that a reasonable reading of the requirements in IFRS accounting standards could result in the application of either one of two approaches to determine ‘cost’: the ‘fair value as deemed cost approach’ or the ‘accumulated cost approach’.3 See # Chapter 9 at section 2.1.1.C. How we see it Given the IFRS IC agenda decision relating to a step acquisition of a subsidiary in separate financial statements, in our view, the following approaches are both acceptable methods of measuring the previously held interest in the associate or joint venture: • Approach 1 Fair value as deemed cost • Approach 2 Equity-accounted carrying amount as accumulated cost Consistent with that agenda decision, any adjustment to remeasure the previously held interest to its fair value meets the definition of income or expense in the Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting and is, therefore, recognised in profit or loss. Approach 1 Fair value as deemed cost Where a ‘fair value as deemed cost approach’ is applied, the fair value of the previously held interest at the date that control is obtained is deemed to be the cost for the purposes of accounting for the acquisition of the subsidiary. Under this approach, the transaction is viewed as if the entity is exchanging its initial interest (plus consideration for the additional interest) for a controlling interest in the investee (‘exchange view’). As noted above, any adjustment to remeasure the previously held interest to its fair value is recognised in profit or loss. Approach 2 Equity-accounted carrying amount as accumulated cost Following an ‘accumulated cost approach’, the carrying amount of the associate or joint venture could be considered the relevant cost in accounting for the acquisition of the subsidiary. Although there is a change in status, it is not appropriate to remeasure the existing ownership interest as the subsidiary is not a business. Accordingly, no gain or loss is recorded on the acquisition of the subsidiary, assuming that the carrying amount of the new subsidiary is recoverable. In certain circumstances, it might be acceptable to take a third approach and consider the cost of the previously held interest to be the accumulated historical cost consideration for the investment in the associate or joint venture. Given the agenda decision of the IFRS IC mentioned above, any difference arising between the previous carrying amount and the accumulated historical costs will be recognised in profit or loss. However, caution needs to be taken with using this approach as it does not always provide relevant information. If the accumulated historical cost consideration was lower than the equity-accounted carrying amount of the previous associate or joint venture, there would be a loss recognised, even though there is no economic loss arising from the transaction. Where the accumulated historical cost consideration exceeds the equity-accounted carrying amount of the previous associate or joint venture, care is needed when using this approach to ensure that the subsidiary is not carried at above its recoverable amount. IFRIC Update, January 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests How we see it In our view, the entity accounts for all amounts previously recognised in OCI in relation to the investment on the same basis as would have been required if the investee had directly disposed of the related assets or liabilities. That is, certain amounts previously recognised in OCI (such as cumulative translation differences relating to a foreign operation) must be recycled to profit or loss upon disposal. See # Chapter 12 at section 9.2. 3.1.2.B Measuring the NCI Illustration 3-2 shows the computation of the NCI and the cost of an asset in the consolidated financial statements where a subsidiary (that does not meet the definition of a business, but only holds an asset) is acquired following the purchase of an additional interest in an equity-accounted joint venture. Illustration 3-2: Acquisition of a subsidiary that is not a business where previously held interest is an equity-accounted joint venture Entity A acquires a 50% interest in Entity C upon formation of a joint venture with Entity B in 20X6. Each venturer contributed CU5 million which was used by C to purchase a building accounted using the cost model in IAS 16. All entities have the same functional currency, currency unit (CU). In 20Y1, cumulative earnings of the joint venture were CU1.5 million and cumulative dividends declared were CU0.5 million. Immediately prior to the following transaction, A has an equity accounted joint venture with a carrying amount of CU5.5 million (CU5m initial investment + 50% × (CU1.5m - CU0.5m)). A and B entered into an arrangement whereby A acquired 30% of the shares of C in exchange for CU4.5 million cash paid to B. Transaction costs incurred by A were CU0.2 million. Following the transaction, A has an 80% controlling interest in the equity shares of C, which holds a single building that is not a business. Assuming that there are no control premiums or other investment premiums or discounts, the fair value of the NCI is CU3 million. The fair value of 100% of C at the acquisition date is CU15 million. Tax effects, if any, are ignored in this example. For an asset acquisition, the cost of the group of assets is the sum of all the consideration given (i.e., the cash paid of CU4.5 million plus the ‘cost’ of the previously held interest in C) and any NCI recognised, together with transaction costs. How should A measure the previously held interest in C? As noted in section 3.1.2.A above, there are two main approaches to determine the ‘cost’ of the previously held interest - Approach 1 and Approach 2. Under Approach 1, A records the previously held interest in C at its fair value of CU7.5 million (50% × CU15m). Accordingly, on acquisition, A records a gain of CU2 million on remeasurement of the previously held interest. Under Approach 2, A measures its previously held interest in C at its previous equity accounted value of CU5.5 million. No gain or loss is recognised on the transaction. How should A measure the NCI in the step acquisition? As noted in section 3.1.1 above, there are four approaches: • Approach (a) Measure NCI based on the proportionate share of the fair values of C’s identifiable net assets • Approach (b) Measure NCI at its fair value • Approach (c) Measure NCI based on the proportionate share of the consolidated book values of C’s net assets (including transaction costs) • Approach (d) Measure NCI based on the proportionate share of the consolidated book values of C’s net assets (excluding transaction costs) Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests A records the following amounts for NCI: Previously held interest - deemed cost Approach 1 (fair value) Approach 2 (equity-accounted value) CU m CU m Fair value of building (i) 15.0 15.0 Deemed cost of previous interest (ii) 7.5 5.5 Consideration paid for second tranche (iii) 4.5 4.5 Transaction costs (iv) 0.2 0.2 Fair value of NCI (v) 3.0 3.0 NCI - Approach (a): 20% of fair value of building (i) 3.0 3.0 NCI - Approach (b): fair value (v) 3.0 3.0 NCI - Approach (c): 20/80 of [(ii) + (iii) + (iv)] 3.1 2.6 NCI - Approach (d): 20/80 of [(ii) + (iii)] 3.0 2.5 The cost of the building consolidated is as follows: Previously held interest - deemed cost Approach 1 Approach 2 (fair value) (equity-accounted value) CU m CU m NCI - Approach (a) 15.2 13.2 NCI - Approach (b) 15.2 13.2 NCI - Approach (c) 15.3 12.8 NCI - Approach (d) 15.2 12.7 The cost of the building comprises the sum of: • The deemed cost of the previously held interest (i.e., CU7.5 million under Approach 1 and CU5.5 million under Approach 2) • The consideration paid for the second tranche of CU4.5 million • Transaction costs of CU0.2 million And • NCI recognised as in the earlier table above 3.2 Accounting for a loss of control IFRS 10 clarifies that an investor is required to reassess whether it controls an investee if the facts and circumstances indicate that there are changes to one or more of the three elements of control. [IFRS 10.8, IFRS 10.B80]. The elements of control are: power over the investee; exposure, or rights, to variable returns from the investor’s involvement with the investee; and the investor’s ability to use its power over the investee to affect the amount of the investor’s returns. [IFRS 10.7]. See Chapter 7 at section 9 for further discussion, including examples of situations where a change in control may arise. A parent may lose control of a subsidiary because of a transaction that changes its absolute or relative ownership level. For example, a parent may lose control of a subsidiary if: • It sells some or all of the ownership interests • It contributes or distributes some or all of the ownership interests • A subsidiary issues new ownership interests to third parties (therefore a dilution in the parent’s interests occurs) Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Alternatively, a parent may lose control without a change in absolute or relative ownership levels. For example, a parent may lose control on expiry of a contractual agreement that previously allowed the parent to control the subsidiary, [IFRS 10.BCZ180], or on entry into a contractual arrangement which gives joint control with another party (or parties). A parent may also lose control if the subsidiary becomes subject to the control of a government, court, administrator, receiver, liquidator, or regulator. This evaluation may require the exercise of judgement, based on the facts and circumstances, including the laws in the relevant jurisdiction (see Chapter 7 at sections 4.3.2.A and 9.2). Extract from IFRS 10 If a parent loses control of a subsidiary, the parent: (a) derecognises the assets and liabilities of the former subsidiary from the consolidated statement of financial position. (b) recognises any investment retained in the former subsidiary at its fair value when control is lost and subsequently accounts for it and for any amounts owed by or to the former subsidiary in accordance with relevant IFRSs. That fair value shall be regarded as the fair value on initial recognition of a financial asset in accordance with IFRS 9 or, when appropriate, the cost on initial recognition of an investment in an associate or joint venture. (c) recognises the gain or loss associated with the loss of control attributable to the former controlling interest. **[IFRS 10.25]** If a parent loses control of a subsidiary, IFRS 10 further specifies that the parent is required to: [IFRS 10.25, **IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98]** • Derecognise the assets (including any goodwill) and liabilities of the former subsidiary at their carrying amounts at the date when control is lost • Derecognise the carrying amount of any NCI in the former subsidiary at the date when control is lost. This includes any components of OCI attributable to them • Recognise the fair value of the consideration received, if any, from the transaction, event or circumstances that resulted in the loss of control • Recognise a distribution if the transaction, event, or circumstances that resulted in the loss of control involves a distribution of shares in the subsidiary to owners in their capacity as owners (see section 3.7 below) • Recognise any investment retained in the former subsidiary at its fair value at the date when control is lost (see section 3.3 below) • Reclassify to profit or loss, or transfer directly to retained earnings if required by other IFRS accounting standards, the amounts recognised in OCI in relation to the subsidiary If a parent loses control of a subsidiary, the parent accounts for all amounts previously recognised in OCI in relation to that subsidiary on the same basis as would be required if the parent had directly disposed of the related assets or liabilities. This is discussed in section 3.5 below. • Recognise any resulting difference as a gain or loss in profit or loss attributable to the parent Any amounts owed to or by the former subsidiary (which cease to be eliminated on consolidation) are accounted for in accordance with the relevant IFRS accounting standards. Such balances are often financial assets or financial liabilities, which are initially recognised at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9 at the date of loss of control. [IFRS 9.5.1.1]. See Chapter 45 at section 3. Sometimes, the parent may receive contingent consideration on the sale of a subsidiary. In most cases, the parent will have a contractual right to receive cash or another financial asset from the purchaser and, therefore, such balances are often financial assets within the scope of IFRS 9, and consequently initially measured at fair value. [IFRS 9.5.1.1]. See also Chapter 41 at section 2.7.1.B. IFRS 5’s requirements apply to a non-current asset (or disposal group) that is classified as held for sale. See # Chapter 5 at section 2. The presentation requirements when the subsidiary of which the parent loses control meets the definition of a discontinued operation are discussed in Chapter 5 at section 3. A proposed transaction that would, if implemented, lead to loss of control of a subsidiary could be an indicator of impairment under IAS 36 Impairment of Assets, or, if the subsidiary is held for sale, under **IFRS 5. [IFRS 10.BCZ186].** # Chapter 21 at section 8.5 addresses the allocation of goodwill when an operation is disposed of which forms part of a cash-generating unit to which goodwill has been allocated. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities requires disclosure of the gain or loss (if any) arising on loss of control of a subsidiary during the reporting period and the line item in which the gain or loss is recognised (if not presented separately). The portion of the gain or loss attributable to measuring any investment retained in the former subsidiary at its fair value (in accordance with IFRS 13) at the date when control is lost must also be disclosed. [IFRS 12.10(b)(iv), IFRS 12.19]. See Chapter 14 at section 4.5.2. Similar disclosures are required where IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures is applied by eligible subsidiaries (see Chapter 4 at section 7 for further details on the requirements of **IFRS 19).4 [IFRS 19.81].** How we see it Applying IFRS 10’s requirements on loss of control of a subsidiary could pose some difficulties, where these interact with the requirements of certain other IFRS accounting standards, such as IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers (sale of a single-asset entity containing real estate), IFRS 16 Leases (sale of a single-asset entity and leaseback of its building) and IAS 28 (sale or contribution of an asset to an associate or joint venture). In our view, this is partly caused by the fact that IFRS 10 refers only to loss of control of a subsidiary, without drawing a distinction between whether the subsidiary is a business or not, or whether the loss of control happens as part of the entity’s ordinary activities or not. The interaction of the above standards with IFRS 10 has been the subject of discussions by the IFRS IC and by the IASB, as described further below. During 2019 and 2020, the IFRS IC and the IASB discussed whether IFRS 10 or IFRS 15 applies when an entity loses control of a single asset entity containing real estate as part of its ordinary activities.5 This is discussed further in section 3.2.1 below. Moreover, during 2020 and 2021, the IFRS IC discussed the interaction of IFRS 10 and IFRS 16 in relation to a transaction where the entity’s equity interest in a wholly- owned subsidiary that held only a building was sold, resulting in loss of control of the former subsidiary, and the building was leased back. This is discussed further in section 3.2.2 below. The conflict between the requirements of IFRS 10 and those of IAS 28 arises because IAS 28 restricts any gain arising on the sale of an asset to an associate or joint venture (or on the contribution of a non- monetary asset in exchange for an equity interest in an associate or a joint venture) to that attributable to the unrelated investors’ interests in the associate or joint venture, whereas IFRS 10 does not. [IAS 28.28, IAS 28.30, IFRS 10.25]. In order to resolve the conflict, in September 2014, the IASB had issued Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture (Amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28) but subsequently deferred the effective date of application of the amendments. This issue is discussed further in sections 3.3.2 and 7.1 below. The following parts of this section address further issues relating to accounting for the loss of control of a subsidiary including: where an interest is retained in the former subsidiary (see section 3.3 below), loss of control in multiple arrangements (see section 3.4 below), the treatment of OCI on loss of control (see section 3.5 below), deemed disposals (see section 3.6 below), and demergers and distributions (see section 3.7 below). 3.2.1 IFRS IC and IASB discussions about the sale of a single asset entity containing real estate In June 2019, the IFRS IC discussed a request about the accounting for a transaction in which an entity, as part of its ordinary activities, enters into a contract with a customer to sell real estate by selling its equity interest in a subsidiary.6 The entity established the subsidiary some time before it enters into the contract with the customer; the subsidiary has one asset (real estate inventory) and a related tax asset or liability. The entity has applied IFRS 10 in consolidating the subsidiary before it loses control of the subsidiary as a result of the transaction with the customer. The IFRS IC discussed this issue but did not issue an agenda decision on this matter. Instead, the issue was referred to the IASB. IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures was issued by the IASB in May 2024 to simplify the preparation of financial statements by eligible subsidiaries. Eligible subsidiaries that elect to apply IFRS 19 are allowed to provide reduced disclosures while still applying the recognition, measurement and presentation requirements in other IFRS accounting standards. IFRS 19 is effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027, with earlier application permitted (subject to jurisdictional endorsement). See Chapter 4 at section 7 for further details on the requirements of IFRS 19. IFRIC Update, June 2019, IASB Update, October 2019, IASB Update, June 2020, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, June 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests In October 2019, the IASB directed its Staff to research the feasibility of narrow-scope standard setting.7 In June 2020, the IASB discussed a possible narrow-scope amendment that would have required an entity to apply IFRS 15, instead of IFRS 10, to sales of subsidiaries where all of the following apply: • The entity contracts with a customer for goods or services that are the output of its ordinary activities in exchange for consideration • The subsidiary contains only inventory and any related income tax asset or liability • The entity retains no interest in the inventory transferred to the customer The Board decided against proposing this amendment.8 During the discussions held by both the IFRS IC and the IASB, a number of members of the IFRS IC and the Board considered that, absent an amendment, IFRS 10 (rather than IFRS 15) would apply to the transaction considered. During the June 2020 discussion, some Board members thought there was merit in a narrow-scope amendment. However, concerns were raised about potential unintended consequences of proposing a narrow-scope amendment without a more comprehensive discussion. Any narrow-scope amendment would affect the scope of both IFRS 10 and IFRS 15 and, as such, Board members wanted to learn more from stakeholders about the need for, and the consequences of such a project.9 However, these considerations were not noted in the relevant IFRIC Update and IASB Update that were issued as summaries of the meetings. This matter was further considered as part of Phase 2 of the post- implementation review (PIR) of IFRS 10 (see section 7.5 below).10 In June 2022, the Board assessed this matter as a low priority and concluded this topic could be explored if identified as a priority in the next agenda consultation (i.e., the Fourth Agenda Consultation, expected to start in 2026).11 Indeed, the Board decided not to include a question on this matter as part of the PIR of IFRS 15.12 In July 2024, the Board confirmed this matter would be considered in the next Agenda Consultation rather than as part of the PIR of IFRS 15.13 This issue is discussed further in Question 2-11 of Chapter 28 at section 2.5. How we see it Even though the deliberation process on this question has been completed, there might continue to be some diversity in practice. However, in the meantime, entities might need to consider the discussions on this matter as they determine the appropriate standard to apply to transactions with customers involving the loss of control of a single asset entity. 3.2.2 IFRS IC discussions about a transaction where the entire equity interest in a subsidiary holding only a single building is sold and the building is leased back In September 2020, a related issue was considered by the IFRS IC concerning the accounting for a sale and leaseback transaction.14 In the fact pattern, the parent sells all its equity interest in a wholly-owned subsidiary, losing control at the date of transfer. The subsidiary was established some time ago and holds only a single asset - a building (which does not constitute a business under IFRS 3) - and has no liabilities. The sales price (equal to the fair value of the building) exceeds the carrying amount of the transferred building, resulting in a gain. As part of the transaction, the entity enters into a contract to lease the building back, paying market rates for the lease rentals. The transfer of the building would meet the requirements to be accounted for as a ‘sale’ under IFRS 15. *Reference: IASB Update, October 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* *Reference: IASB Update, June 2020; and IASB Staff Paper 12A, Project: Maintenance and Consistent Application, Paper topic: Sale of a* Subsidiary to a Customer, IASB Meeting, June 2020. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Project summary: Sale of a Subsidiary to a Customer, IFRS Foundation’s website (accessed 26 September 2024). Request for Information: Post-implementation Review: IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements, IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements and IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities, IASB, December 2020. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Project Report and Feedback Statement: Post-implementation Review: IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements, IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements, IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities, IASB, June 2022. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Request for Information: Post-implementation review of IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers, June 2023, Spotlight 9.4, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. *Reference: IASB Update, July 2024, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* IFRIC Update, September 2020, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests The question posed to the IFRS IC was whether the IFRS 16 sale and leaseback guidance applies, [IFRS 16.100(a)], (and therefore the entity recognises only the amount of the gain that relates to the rights transferred to the third party - see # Chapter 24 at section 8) or whether the transaction is accounted for as a loss of control of a subsidiary in accordance with IFRS 10. [IFRS 10.25, IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99]. Under IFRS 10, a gain on loss of control would be recognised and then the parent would account for the lease entered into with the third party. The IFRS IC noted that, in the transaction described in the request, the entity loses control of the subsidiary. Accordingly, the loss of control requirements in IFRS 10 apply to the transaction. In addition, the entity transfers the building to the third party (through the sale of its equity interest in the subsidiary) and leases the building back. The transaction is therefore a sale and leaseback transaction as described in IFRS 16.98, to which the sale and leaseback requirements in IFRS 16 apply. [IFRS 16.98]. The IFRS IC therefore tentatively concluded that, in the transaction described in the request, the entity is required to apply IFRS 10.25 and IFRS 10.B97-B99 to account for the loss of control of the subsidiary. In particular, IFRS 10.B98 requires the entity to derecognise the building held by the subsidiary and recognise the fair value of the consideration received. [IFRS 10.25, IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B97, IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99]. Since the transfer of the building satisfies the requirements in IFRS 15 to be accounted for as a sale of the building (the IFRS IC observed that this does not mean that the transaction is within the scope of IFRS 15), [IFRS 16.99], the entity must also apply IFRS 16.100(a). Therefore, the entity (a) measures the right-of-use asset arising from the leaseback at the proportion of the previous carrying amount of the building that relates to the right of use it retains; and (b) recognises only the amount of any gain that relates to the rights transferred to the third party. The entity also recognises a liability at the date of the transaction, the initial measurement of which is a consequence of how the right-of-use asset is measured and the gain on the sale and leaseback transaction determined. Consequently, the gain recognised by the entity on the transaction reflects the requirements in IFRS 16.100(a). [IFRS 16.100(a)]. The IFRS IC received a number of comment letters on the tentative agenda decision that raised concerns about the accounting requirements.15 Therefore, in February 2021, the IFRS IC decided to refer the matter to the IASB for further consideration of narrow-scope standard-setting. At the time of writing, the Board has not yet discussed this issue.16 As noted in section 3.2.1 above, transactions involving corporate wrappers were further considered as part of Phase 2 of the PIR of IFRS 10 (see section 7.5 below).17 In June 2022, the Board assessed transactions involving corporate wrappers as a low priority and concluded this topic could be explored if identified as a priority in the next agenda consultation (i.e., the Fourth Agenda Consultation expected to start in 2026).18 How we see it In the absence of further guidance, we believe entities should consider the views of local regulators before establishing an initial accounting policy for transactions involving the transfer of a controlling interest in an entity that holds an underlying asset that will be leased back to the transferor. Entities also need to carefully consider the disclosure of these transactions to ensure that users of the financial statements can assess the impact. The disclosure requirements for sale and leaseback transactions are discussed in Chapter 24 at section 8.4. 3.3 Accounting for a loss of control where an interest is retained in the former subsidiary IFRS 10 requires that when a parent loses control of a subsidiary, it must recognise any investment retained in the former subsidiary at its fair value at the date when control is lost. Any gain or loss on the transaction will be recorded in profit or loss. The fair value of any investment that it retains at the date control is lost will be accounted for, as applicable, as: [IFRS 10.25(b)] • The fair value on initial recognition of a financial asset (see Chapter 45 at section 3) Or • The cost on initial recognition of an investment in an associate or joint venture (see Chapter 12 at section 6.2.2) *Reference: IASB Staff Paper 2: Project: Sale and Leaseback of an Asset in a Single-Asset Entity (IFRS 10 and IFRS 16), Paper topic: Comment* letters on tentative agenda decision, IFRS IC meeting, February 2021. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, February 2021; and IASB pipeline projects, IFRS Foundation’s website (accessed 26 September 2024). Request for Information: Post-implementation Review: IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements, IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements and IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities, IASB, December 2020. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Project Report and Feedback Statement: Post-implementation Review: IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements, IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements, IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities, IASB, June 2022. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests As noted in section 3.2 above, any amounts owed to or by the former subsidiary (which cease to be eliminated on consolidation subsequent to the loss of control of the former subsidiary) are accounted for in accordance with the relevant IFRS accounting standards. Such balances are often financial assets or financial liabilities, which are initially recognised at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9 at the date of loss of control. [IFRS 9.5.1.1]. IAS 28 also clarifies that an entity applies IFRS 9 to other financial instruments in an associate or joint venture to which the equity method is not applied. These include long-term interests that, in substance, form part of the entity’s net investment in an associate or joint venture although the standard explains the interaction of its requirements on recognition of losses under the equity method and impairment, where there are such long-term interests. [IAS 28.14A, IAS 28.38, IAS 28.40-43]. See Chapter 12 at section 10.3 and Chapter 45 at section 3. The IASB’s view is that the loss of control of a subsidiary is a significant economic event that marks the end of the previous parent-subsidiary relationship and the start of a new investor-investee relationship, which is recognised and measured initially at the date when control is lost. IFRS 10 is based on the premise that an investor-investee relationship differs significantly from a parent-subsidiary relationship. Therefore, the *Reference: IASB decided that “any investment the parent has in the former subsidiary after control is lost should be* measured at fair value at the date that control is lost and that any resulting gain or loss should be recognised in profit or loss.” [IFRS 10.BCZ182]. The following discussion addresses the accounting for the loss of control in certain situations: • The interest retained in the former subsidiary is a financial asset (see section 3.3.1 below) • The interest retained in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture that is accounted for using the equity method (see section 3.3.2 below) • The interest retained in the former subsidiary is a joint operation (see section 3.3.3 below) 3.3.1 Interest retained in the former subsidiary - financial asset Illustration 3-3 below demonstrates the above requirement where the interest retained in the former subsidiary is a financial asset. Illustration 3-3: Disposal of a subsidiary with a retained interest that is a financial asset A parent sells an 85% interest in a wholly-owned subsidiary as follows: • After the sale the parent elects to account for the interest at fair value through other comprehensive income (FVOCI) under IFRS 9 • The subsidiary did not recognise any amounts in OCI • Net assets of the subsidiary before the disposal are CU500 million • Cash proceeds from the sale of the 85% interest are CU750 million • The fair value of the 15% interest retained by the parent is CU130 million The parent accounts for the disposal of an 85% interest as follows: CU m CU m Dr Financial asset Dr Cash Cr Net assets of the subsidiary derecognised (summarised) Cr Gain on loss of control of subsidiary The gain recognised on the loss of control of the subsidiary is calculated as follows: CU m CU m Gain on interest disposed of Cash proceeds on disposal of 85% interest Carrying amount of 85% interest (85% × CU500m) (425) Gain on interest retained Carrying amount of 15% investment carried at FVOCI Carrying amount of 15% interest (15% × CU500m) (75) Gain recognised on loss of control of subsidiary Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Although IFRS 10 requires that any investment retained in the former subsidiary is to be recognised at its fair value at the date when control is lost, no guidance is given in the standard as to how such fair value is to be determined. However, IFRS 13 provides detailed guidance on how fair value is determined for financial reporting purposes. IFRS 13 is discussed in detail in Chapter 15. 3.3.2 Interest retained in the former subsidiary - associate or joint venture It can be seen that the requirements in IFRS 10 discussed above result in a gain or loss arising on loss of control as if the parent had sold all of its interest in the subsidiary, not just that relating to the percentage interest that has been sold. IFRS 10’s requirements also apply where a parent loses control over a subsidiary that has become an associate or a joint venture. However, there is a conflict between these requirements and those of IAS 28 for transactions where a parent sells or contributes an interest in a subsidiary to an associate or a joint venture (accounted for using the equity method) and the sale or contribution results in a loss of control in the subsidiary by the parent. This is because IAS 28 restricts any gain arising on the sale of an asset to an associate or a joint venture, or on the contribution of a non-monetary asset in exchange for an equity interest in an associate or a joint venture, to that attributable to unrelated investors’ interests in the associate or joint venture. [IAS 28.28, IAS 28.30]. In order to resolve the conflict, in September 2014, the IASB had issued Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture (Amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28) (the September 2014 amendments). These amendments, where applied, would require the gain or loss resulting from the loss of control of a subsidiary that does not contain a business (as defined in IFRS 3, [IFRS 3.3, IFRS 3 Appendix A, IFRS 3.B7-B12D] - see Chapter 10 at section 3.2) - as a result of a sale or contribution of a subsidiary to an associate or a joint venture accounted for using the equity method - to be recognised only to the extent of the unrelated investors’ interests in the associate or joint venture. The same requirement applies to the remeasurement gain or loss relating to the parent’s retained interest in the former subsidiary if, following the transaction, the former subsidiary is now an associate or a joint venture that is accounted for using the equity method. [IFRS 10.25, IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B99A]. See Illustration 3-4 in section 3.3.2.B below. However, a full gain or loss would be recognised on the loss of control of a subsidiary that constitutes a business, including cases in which the investor retains joint control of, or significant influence over, the investee. [IFRS 10.25, IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99]. IFRS 10, prior to the revisions resulting from the September 2014 amendments, does not draw a distinction in accounting for the loss of control of a subsidiary that is a business and one that is not. The September 2014 amendments were to be applied prospectively to transactions occurring in annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2016, with earlier application permitted. However, in December 2015, the IASB issued a further amendment, Effective Date of Amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28. This amendment deferred the effective date of the September 2014 amendment indefinitely. [IFRS 10.C1C, IAS 28.45C]. Nevertheless, the IASB has continued to allow early application of the September 2014 amendments as it did not wish to prohibit the application of better financial reporting. [IFRS 10.BC190N, **IFRS 10.BC190O, IAS 28.BC37I, IAS 28.BC37J].** The IASB commenced its research project on the equity method in 2020 and issued an Exposure Draft addressing application questions relating to the equity method in September 202419 (see section 7.1 below). The Exposure Draft proposes that an investor, in applying IAS 28, would recognise the full gain or loss on all transactions with its associate and improvements for the disclosure requirements when an investor recognises the full gain or loss on transactions with its associate, consistent with the tentative decisions made during the research project.20 This is a different position to the September 2014 amendments. The accounting treatment where the September 2014 amendments are not applied is explained in section 3.3.2.A below, whereas section 3.3.2.B below addresses the accounting treatment where the September 2014 amendments are applied. In these sections, IFRS 10’s approach is also referred to as ‘full gain recognition’ whereas IAS 28’s approach is also referred to as ‘partial gain recognition’. Sections 3.3.2.C to 3.3.2.G below explain additional issues relevant to loss of control transactions where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method, whether or not the September 2014 amendments are applied (except where stated). Exposure Draft IASB/ED/2024/7 Equity Method of Accounting - IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures, IASB, September 2024, paras. 53 to 54. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. *Reference: IASB Update, March 2023, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* ## Contents - 6.2.2.A and 6.2.2.B. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 3.3.2.A Conflict between IFRS 10 and IAS 28 (September 2014 amendments not applied) Examples of situations that result in the loss of control of a subsidiary, where the interest retained in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method, include: • Scenario 1 - a ‘downstream’ sale or contribution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is a business) to an existing associate or joint venture • Scenario 2 - a ‘downstream’ sale or contribution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is not a business) to an existing associate or joint venture • Scenario 3 - a direct sale or dilution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is a business) for cash in a transaction involving a third party with a retained interest in an associate or joint venture • Scenario 4 - a direct sale or dilution of the investment in the subsidiary (which is not a business) for cash in a transaction involving a third party with a retained interest in an associate or joint venture While the discussion below refers to these scenarios for simplicity, the same principles apply where the consideration received is not cash, for example, receipt of non-cash assets such as an interest in a subsidiary, or the assumption of liabilities. The scenarios above consider existing associates or joint ventures. Transactions involving the formation of a joint venture, with contributions (which could include an investment in a subsidiary) from the venturers, are discussed further in Chapter 12 at section 7.5.3. [IAS 28.28, IAS 28.29, IAS 28.30, IAS 28.31]. While that chapter discusses the formation of a joint venture, similar accounting would be expected on the establishment of an associate with contributions from the investors. See also Chapter 12 at sections 6.2.2, In determining the accounting under the scenarios listed above prior to applying the September 2014 amendments, it is important first to determine whether the transactions fall within scope of IFRS 10’s requirements on loss of control of a subsidiary (see section 3.2 above) or IAS 28’s requirements on ‘downstream transactions’ with an associate or joint venture accounted using the equity method. Extract from IAS 28 Gains and losses resulting from ‘upstream’ and ‘downstream’ transactions between an entity (including its consolidated subsidiaries) and its associate or joint venture are recognised in the entity’s financial statements only to the extent of unrelated investors’ interests in the associate or joint venture. ‘Upstream’ transactions are, for example, sales of assets from an associate or a joint venture to the investor. ‘Downstream’ transactions are, for example, sales or contributions of assets from the investor to its associate or its joint venture. The investor’s share in the associate’s or joint venture’s gains or losses resulting from these transactions is eliminated. When downstream transactions provide evidence of a reduction in the net realisable value of the assets to be sold or contributed, or of an impairment loss of those assets, those losses shall be recognised in full by the investor. … These requirements in IAS 28 only apply to ‘downstream’ transactions with an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method and not to all transactions where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method. As noted in section 3.3.2 above, IFRS 10.25, prior to the revisions resulting from the September 2014 amendments, does not draw a distinction in accounting for the loss of control of a subsidiary that is a business and one that is not. The September 2014 amendments (and indeed other pronouncements, such as Accounting for Acquisitions of Interests in Joint Operations (Amendments to IFRS 11) issued May 2014), however, do set out different accounting treatments depending on whether an entity is a business or not. How we see it In our view, an entity is not precluded from applying IFRS 10.25, i.e., full gain recognition (see section 3.2 above), in accounting for the loss of control of a subsidiary that is not a business.
