We believe that entities not applying the September 2014 amendments are entitled to make an accounting policy determination as to whether IFRS 10.25 applies to all transactions involving a loss of control of a subsidiary (‘wide view’) or only to such transactions where the subsidiary is a business (‘narrow view’). Where a transaction falls in scope of both IFRS 10.25 and IAS 28.28, we believe that there is a choice of which accounting standards takes precedence, to be applied consistently. Where a transaction falls outside the scope of both standards, an appropriate accounting policy must be determined, with regard to the requirements of IAS 8. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Therefore, in our view, the following accounting policy determinations are needed: (a) Do the requirements of IFRS 10.25 apply to: • A loss of control of a subsidiary that is a business (‘narrow view’) Or • A loss of control of a subsidiary (whether a business or not) (‘wide view’)? (b) Where there is a conflict between the requirements of IFRS 10.25 (‘IFRS 10 approach’) and IAS 28.28 (‘IAS 28 approach’), which accounting standard takes precedence? (c) Where the transaction falls within the scope of neither IFRS 10.25 nor IAS 28.28, what accounting policy is applied? Scenarios 1 and 3 fall within the scope of IFRS 10.25, whether a ‘wide view’ or ‘narrow view’ is taken for accounting policy determination (a) above. However, Scenarios 2 and 4 will only fall within the scope of IFRS 10 if a ‘wide view’ is taken. Only Scenarios 1 and 2 fall within the scope of IAS 28.28. This is because this requirement in IAS 28 only applies to ‘downstream’ transactions with an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method and not to all transactions where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method. Both the IFRS IC and IASB have discussed the accounting for certain transactions, where there was a question over the interaction of IFRS 10 with IFRS 15 (see section 3.2.1 above) and with IFRS 16 (see section 3.2.2 above). These discussions may have an impact on the accounting policy determinations outlined above. How we see it Until such time as the IASB issues further pronouncements, there is likely to continue to be diversity of practice. However, in the meantime, considering the discussions on the interplay of IFRS 10 with other standards such as IFRS 15 and IFRS 16 above, entities may need to be mindful when applying a ‘narrow view’ of the scope of IFRS 10.25 as mentioned above. Figure 3-1 below summarises how the above policy determinations apply to the four scenarios where the September 2014 amendments are not applied. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Figure 3-1: Loss of control transactions where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted for using the equity method Subsidiary meets the definition of a business Subsidiary does not meet the definition of a business Sale or contribution of investment in former subsidiary in downstream transaction with existing associate or joint venture Scenario 1 • Both IFRS 10.25 (as the subsidiary is a business) and IAS 28.28 (as this is a ‘downstream’ transaction) apply to this scenario, so a conflict arises. • An entity would need to develop an accounting policy to resolve the conflict, which would result in either applying full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) or partial gain recognition (IAS 28 approach). Scenario 2 • IAS 28.28 (as this is a ‘downstream’ transaction) applies to this scenario. Therefore, partial gain recognition applies. • However, if a ‘wide view’ is taken on the scope of IFRS 10.25, a conflict arises. An entity would then need to develop an accounting policy to resolve the conflict, which would result in either applying full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) or partial gain recognition (IAS 28 approach). Sale or dilution of investment in former subsidiary for cash in transaction with third party Scenario 3 • IAS 28.28 does not apply (as this is not a ‘downstream’ transaction). • IFRS 10.25 applies (as the subsidiary is a business). • Therefore, an entity applies full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) to the transaction. Scenario 4 • IAS 28.28 does not apply (as this is not a ‘downstream transaction’). • IFRS 10.25 only applies if a ‘wide view’ is taken. • Therefore, if a ‘wide view’ over the scope of IFRS 10.25 applies, an entity applies full gain recognition (IFRS 10 approach) to the transaction. • If a ‘narrow view’ over the scope of IFRS 10.25 applies, an entity must develop and apply an appropriate accounting policy that provides relevant and reliable information in accordance with IAS 8. How we see it Where it is determined that a loss of control transaction falls within the scope of neither IFRS 10.25 nor IAS 28.28 (which might be the case for Scenario 4 if a ‘narrow view’ of the scope of IFRS 10.25 is taken), management will need to use judgement in developing and applying an appropriate accounting policy that results in relevant and reliable information, which must be consistently applied. Some transactions that fall within Scenarios 1 and 3, and in Scenarios 2 and 4 might be similar in substance, which could be a relevant consideration when developing an appropriate accounting policy. Nevertheless, different accounting outcomes could arise depending on the accounting policy determinations made by the entity. In our view, accounting policies must be applied consistently to like transactions. This does not preclude a different choice of which standard takes precedence in Scenario 1 to the choice made for transactions in Scenario 2. An entity only needs to make a determination as to whether a ‘wide view’ or ‘narrow view’ of the scope of IFRS 10.25 applies, if it enters into a transaction that falls within Scenarios 2 or 4 (because Scenarios 1 and 3 always fall within that paragraph’s scope). However, once that determination has been required to be made, the ‘wide view’ or ‘narrow view’ must be applied consistently. However, a different accounting policy could be adopted for transactions that are businesses and transactions that are not businesses (even where a ‘wide view’ of the scope of IFRS 10.25 is taken). For example, an entity taking the ‘wide view’ might consider it appropriate that IFRS 10 takes precedence in Scenario 1 (where the subsidiary is a business) but IAS 28 takes precedence in Scenario 2 (where the subsidiary is not a business). This is because transactions involving the loss of control of a subsidiary that is a business have a different nature to those involving a subsidiary that is not a business and an entity might conclude that partial gain recognition is more appropriate for the loss of control of a subsidiary that is not a business. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Note that if the September 2014 amendments (discussed in sections 3.3.2.B and 7.1 below) were applied, full gain recognition would be required in Scenario 1, whereas partial gain recognition would be required in Scenario 2. 3.3.2.B Conflict between IFRS 10 and IAS 28 (September 2014 amendments applied) As noted in section 3.3.2 above, in September 2014, the IASB had issued Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture (Amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28). Although the mandatory application of the September 2014 amendments was subsequently deferred, the amendments remain available for early application. However, these amendments have not been endorsed for use in all jurisdictions. In the Basis for Conclusions, the IASB clarified that the amendments do not apply where a transaction with a third party leads to a loss of control (even if the retained interest in the former subsidiary becomes an associate or joint venture that is accounted for using the equity method), nor where the investor elects to measure its investments in associates or joint ventures at fair value in accordance with IFRS 9. **[IFRS 10.BC190I].** How we see it The September 2014 amendments regarding the sale or contribution of assets between an investor and its associate or joint venture impact the accounting for situations where there was a conflict between IAS 28 and IFRS 10 (i.e., for Scenarios 1 and 2 discussed above), as discussed in section 3.3.2.A above. The amendments do not specifically address the accounting for situations where such a conflict does not directly exist (i.e., Scenarios 3 and 4 discussed above), and the analysis included in section 3.3.2.A above remains relevant to such situations. The September 2014 amendments added the guidance explained in the following paragraph in relation to the accounting for the loss of control of a subsidiary that does not contain a business. [IFRS 10.25, IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B99A]. Consequently, on the loss of control of a subsidiary that constitutes a business, including cases in which the investor retains joint control of, or significant influence over, the investee, the guidance below is not to be applied. In such cases, the full gain or loss determined under the requirements of IFRS 10.B98 and B99 (see section 3.2 above) is to be recognised. [IFRS 10.25, IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99]. Corresponding amendments were made to IAS 28 to clarify that partial gain recognition would apply if the sale or contribution to the associate or joint venture involved assets that do not constitute a business. [IAS 28.28, IAS 28.30, IAS 28.31A, IAS 28.31B]. See Chapter 12 at section 6.2.2. Extract from IFRS 10 (as amended by September 2014 amendments) B99A If a parent loses control of a subsidiary that does not contain a business, as defined in IFRS 3, as a result of a transaction involving an associate or a joint venture that is accounted for using the equity method, the parent determines the gain or loss in accordance with paragraphs B98- B99. The gain or loss resulting from the transaction (including the amounts previously recognised in other comprehensive income that would be reclassified to profit or loss in accordance with paragraph B99) is recognised in the parent’s profit or loss only to the extent of the unrelated investors’ interests in that associate or joint venture. The remaining part of the gain is eliminated against the carrying amount of the investment in that associate or joint venture. In addition, if the parent retains an investment in the former subsidiary and the former subsidiary is now an associate or a joint venture that is accounted for using the equity method, the parent recognises the part of the gain or loss resulting from the remeasurement at fair value of the investment retained in that former subsidiary in its profit or loss only to the extent of the unrelated investors’ interests in the new associate or joint venture. The remaining part of that gain is eliminated against the carrying amount of the investment retained in the former subsidiary. If the parent retains an investment in the former subsidiary that is now accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9, the part of the gain or loss resulting from the remeasurement at fair value of the investment retained in the former subsidiary is recognised in full in the parent’s profit or loss. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests An example, based on one provided by IFRS 10 illustrating the application of this guidance, is reflected in Illustration 3-4 below.21 Illustration 3-4: Loss of control of a subsidiary that does not contain a business as a result of a transaction involving an associate A parent has a 100% interest in a subsidiary that does not contain a business. The parent sells 70% of its interest in the subsidiary to an associate in which it has a 20% interest. As a consequence of this transaction, the parent loses control of the subsidiary. The carrying amount of the net assets of the subsidiary is CU100 and the carrying amount of the interest sold is CU70 (CU100 × 70%). The fair value of the consideration received is CU210, which is also the fair value of the interest sold. The investment retained in the former subsidiary is an associate accounted for using the equity method and its fair value is CU90. The gain determined in accordance with IFRS 10.B98-B99 is CU200 and after the elimination required by IFRS 10.B99A amounts to CU146. [IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99, IFRS 10.B99A]. This gain comprises two parts: (a) A gain of CU140 resulting from the sale of the 70% interest in the subsidiary to the associate. This gain is the difference between the fair value of the consideration received (CU210) and the carrying amount of the interest sold (CU70). In accordance with IFRS 10.B99A, the parent recognises in its profit or loss the amount of the gain attributable to the unrelated investors’ interests in the existing associate. [IFRS 10.B99A]. This is 80% of this gain, which is CU112 (CU140 × 80%). The remaining 20% of the gain (CU28 = CU140 × 20%), is eliminated against the carrying amount of the investment in the existing associate. (b) A gain of CU60 resulting from the remeasurement at fair value of the investment directly retained in the former subsidiary. This gain is the difference between the fair value of the investment retained in the former subsidiary (CU90) and 30% of the carrying amount of the net assets of the subsidiary (CU30=CU100 × 30%). In accordance with IFRS 10.B99A, the parent recognises in its profit or loss the amount of the gain attributable to the unrelated investors’ interests in the new associate. This is 56% (70% × 80%) of the gain, which is CU34 (CU60 × 56%). [IFRS 10.B99A]. The remaining 44% of the gain (CU26 = CU60 × 44%) is eliminated against the carrying amount of the investment retained in the former subsidiary. This example would therefore give rise to the following journal, assuming cash consideration of CU210: CU CU Dr Cash Dr Investment in new associate (former subsidiary) (see (1) below) Cr Gain on disposal (see (2) below) Cr Net assets of former subsidiary (previously consolidated) Cr Investment in existing associate (1) This represents the fair value of the investment in the former subsidiary (now an associate) of CU90 less the eliminated gain of CU26 (see (b) above). (2) This represents the gain of CU200 less the eliminated gains of CU28 (eliminated against the investment in the associate - see (a) above) and CU26 (eliminated against the investment in the new associate/former subsidiary - see (1) above). 3.3.2.C Reclassification of items of OCI where the interest retained in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted using the equity method As discussed in section 3.2 above, on loss of control of a subsidiary, an entity must reclassify to profit or loss, or transfer directly to retained earnings if required by other IFRS accounting standards, the amounts recognised in OCI in relation to the subsidiary (see section 3.5 below). Where the retained interest in the former subsidiary is an associate or joint venture accounted using the equity method, the question arises as to whether amounts recognised in OCI that are required to be reclassified to profit or loss are reclassified in full or in part. How we see it In our view, where an IFRS 10 approach is applied, OCI reclassified is recognised in full on loss of control of the subsidiary, consistent with the full gain approach. While IAS 28 does not explicitly address this issue, we believe that where an IAS 28 approach is applied, partial gain reclassification of OCI applies such that only the proportion attributable to unrelated investors’ interests in the associate or joint venture is reclassified. This treatment is consistent with the September 2014 amendments (see section 3.3.2.B above) but is also applicable where the September 2014 amendments are not applied. Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture (Amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28), *Reference: IASB, September 2014, IFRS 10, paras. 25, 26, B99A, Example 17.* Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 3.3.2.D Application of partial gain recognition where the gain exceeds the carrying amount of the investment in the associate or joint venture accounted using the equity method Occasionally, upon sale or contribution of a subsidiary to an associate or joint venture (that is accounted for using the equity method), the investor’s share of the gain exceeds the carrying value of the investment in the associate or joint venture. Where an IAS 28 approach (partial gain recognition) is applied, the question arises as to what extent is any profit in excess of the carrying value of the investment eliminated. This is addressed in Chapter 12 at section 7.1.1. 3.3.2.E Examples of accounting for sales or contributions to an existing associate (where the September 2014 amendments are not applied) Scenario 1 is demonstrated in Illustration 3-5 below, showing both an IFRS 10 and an IAS 28 approach (a choice between these approaches is permitted where the September 2014 amendments are not applied). As noted in section 3.3.2.B above, an IFRS 10 approach is required for Scenario 1 where the September 2014 amendments are applied. Scenario 3 (where the IFRS 10 approach is required) is demonstrated in Illustration 3-9 in section 3.6 below (for a deemed disposal) and in Illustration 6-1 in Chapter 12 at section 6.2.2.A (a direct sale to a third party). The scenarios referred to in this paragraph are those set out in section 3.3.2.A above. Illustration 3-5: Sale of a subsidiary to an existing associate Parent P owns a 60% interest in its subsidiary S (which is a business). The carrying value of S’s net identifiable assets in the consolidated financial statements of P is CU120 million. P measured the NCI using the proportionate share of net assets; therefore, the NCI is CU48 million (40% of CU120 million). In addition, goodwill of CU15 million was recognised upon the original acquisition of S and has not subsequently been impaired. The goodwill is allocated to S for the purposes of impairment testing. Subsequently, P sells its interest in S to its associate A, in which it has a 40% interest, for CU96 million cash, being the fair value of its 60% interest in S. Therefore, P has a 40% interest in the enlarged associate A (which now holds the 60% interest in S). If the September 2014 amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28 have not been early adopted, either a full gain is recognised (IFRS 10 approach), or the gain is restricted to that attributable to the other investors in A (IAS 28 approach). See section 3.3.2.A above. If the September 2014 amendments have been early adopted, the full gain is recognised as S is a business. See section 3.3.2.B above. IFRS 10 approach This results in a gain of CU9 million on disposal, recognised as follows: CU m CU m Dr Cash 96.0 Dr NCI 48.0 Cr Gain on disposal 9.0 Cr Net assets of S (previously consolidated) 120.0 Cr Goodwill (previously shown separately) 15.0 IAS 28 approach Where the September 2014 amendments are not early adopted and the gain recognised is restricted to the 60% attributable to the other investors in A, a gain of CU5.4 million on disposal is recognised (with the remaining CU3.6 million adjusted against the carrying amount of the investment in A), as follows: CU m CU m Dr Cash 96.0 Dr NCI 48.0 Cr Gain on disposal 5.4 Cr Investment in A 3.6 Cr Net assets of S (previously consolidated) 120.0 Cr Goodwill (previously shown separately) 15.0 Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 3.3.2.F Determination of the fair value of the retained interest in a former subsidiary which is an associate or joint venture As indicated in section 3.3.1 above, no guidance is given in IFRS 10 as to how the fair value of the retained interest in the former subsidiary should be determined. However, IFRS 13 provides detailed guidance on how fair value is to be determined for financial reporting purposes. IFRS 13 is discussed in detail in Chapter 15. One particular issue that has been discussed by the IASB, which might be relevant in determining the fair value of a retained interest that is an associate or a joint venture, is the unit of account for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates. In September 2014, the IASB issued an Exposure Draft that proposed among other matters, the following clarifications to the requirements for measuring fair value, in accordance with IFRS 13, for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates: • The unit of account for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates would be the investment as whole • When a quoted price in an active market is available for the individual financial instruments that comprise the entire investment, the fair value measurement would be the product of the quoted price of the financial instrument (P) multiplied by the quantity (Q) of instruments held (i.e., P × Q)22 During 2015, the IASB continued its deliberations on these proposals and decided that further research should be undertaken with respect to the fair value measurement of investments in subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures that are quoted in an active market.23 This topic was considered further in the PIR of IFRS 13. The IASB concluded that IFRS 13 is working as intended and that the project was complete with no further work required. Although the Project Report and Feedback Statement noted that many stakeholders expressed a view that the IASB should clarify how IFRS 13 deals with the issue of prioritising the unit of account or Level 1 inputs, the IASB decided that it would conduct no other follow up activities in this area (because the costs of further work would exceed its benefits).24 These issues are discussed further in Chapter 15 at sections 1.3 and 4.1.1. 3.3.2.G Presentation of comparative information for a former subsidiary that becomes an investee accounted for using the equity method Where a parent loses control of a subsidiary, so that the former subsidiary becomes an associate or a joint venture accounted for using the equity method, the effect is that the former parent/investor’s interest in the investee is reported: • Using the equity method from the date on which control is lost in the current reporting period • Using full consolidation for any earlier part of the current reporting period, and of any earlier reporting period, during which the investee was controlled It is not acceptable for an entity to restate financial information for reporting periods prior to the loss of control using the equity method to provide comparability with the new presentation. Consolidation continues until control is lost, [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B88], and equity accounting starts only from the date on which an entity becomes an associate or joint venture, [IAS 28.32], (see Chapter 12 at section 6.1). 3.3.3 Interest retained in the former subsidiary - joint operation In some transactions, it is possible that an entity would lose control of a subsidiary but retain an interest in a joint operation to be accounted for under IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements. For example, a parent might contribute an existing business to a newly created joint operation and obtain joint control of the combined operation. Alternatively, it could be achieved by a parent with a 100% subsidiary selling a 50% interest to another party, with the transaction resulting in the formation of a joint operation, with each party having a 50% share of the assets and liabilities of the joint operation. Exposure Draft ED/2014/4, Measuring Quoted Investments in Subsidiaries, Joint Ventures and Associates at Fair Value (Proposed amendments to IFRS 10, IFRS 12, IAS 27, IAS 28 and IAS 36 and Illustrative Examples for IFRS 13), IASB, September 2014. *Reference: IASB Update, July 2015, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* *Reference: IASB Update, March 2018; and IFRS Project Report and Feedback Statement: Post-implementation Review of IFRS 13 Fair value* Measurement, IASB, December 2018. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests As set out in section 3.2 above, in accounting for a loss of control of a subsidiary, a parent is required, among other matters, to: • Derecognise the assets and liabilities of the subsidiary • Recognise any investment retained in the former subsidiary at fair value at the date when control is lost • Recognise any resulting gain or loss in profit or loss However, it is unclear how these requirements are to be applied when the retained interest is in the assets and liabilities of a joint operation. One view is that the retained interest is remeasured at fair value. Another view is that the retained interest is not derecognised or remeasured at fair value, but continues to be recognised and measured at its carrying amount. In July 2016, the IFRS IC discussed whether an entity should remeasure its retained interest in the assets and liabilities of a joint operation when the entity loses control of a business, or an asset or group of assets that is not a business. In the transaction discussed, the entity either retains joint control of a joint operation or is a party to a joint operation (with rights to assets and obligations for liabilities) after the transaction. The IFRS IC noted that IFRS 11.B34-B35 specify that an entity recognises gains or losses on the sale or contribution of assets to a joint operation only to the extent of the other parties’ interests in the joint operation. [IFRS 11.22, IFRS 11.B34, IFRS 11.B35]. The requirements in these paragraphs could be viewed as conflicting with the requirements in IFRS 10, which specify that an entity remeasures any retained interest when it loses control of a subsidiary. The IFRS IC observed that the IASB had issued amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28 in September 2014 to address the accounting for the sale or contribution of assets to an associate or a joint venture. Those amendments (which are discussed in section 3.3.2.B above and section 7.1 below) address a similar conflict that exists between the requirements in IFRS 10 and IAS 28. The IASB decided to defer the effective date of the amendments to IFRS 10 and IAS 28 and further consider a number of related issues at a later date. Because of the similarity between the transaction discussed by the IFRS IC and a sale or contribution of assets to an associate or a joint venture (see section 3.3.2 above), the IFRS IC concluded that the accounting for the two types of transactions should be considered concurrently by the IASB. Consequently, the IFRS IC decided not to add this issue to its agenda but, instead, to recommend that the IASB consider the issue at the same time that it further considers the accounting for the sale or contribution of assets to an associate or a joint venture.25 The interaction of IFRS 10 and IAS 28 has subsequently been considered as part of the IASB’s equity method project (see section 7.1 below). How we see it Where an entity loses control of a former subsidiary, and retains an interest in a joint operation, there is a conflict between the requirements of IFRS 10 and IFRS 11 which means it is unclear whether remeasurement of the retained interest to its fair value is required or allowed. Until the IASB issues further guidance, we believe that, where a parent loses control over a subsidiary but retains an interest in a joint operation that is a business, entities have an accounting policy choice as to whether to remeasure the retained interest at fair value or not. 3.4 Loss of control in multiple arrangements IFRS 10 only allows a parent to recognise a gain or loss on disposal of a subsidiary when the parent loses control over it. This requirement could present opportunities to structure the disposal in a series of disposals, thereby potentially reducing the loss recognised. Illustration 3-6 below, provides an example, based on one included in the Basis for Conclusions to IFRS 10. [IFRS 10.BCZ185]. IFRIC Update, July 2016, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 3-6: Step-disposal of a subsidiary (1) A parent controls 70% of a subsidiary. The parent intends to sell all of its 70% controlling interest in the subsidiary. The parent could structure the disposal in two different ways: • The parent could initially sell 19% of its ownership interest without loss of control and then, soon afterwards, sell the remaining 51% and lose control Or • The parent could sell its entire 70% interest in one transaction In the first case, any difference between the amount by which the NCI are adjusted and the fair value of the consideration received upon sale of the 19% interest would be recognised directly in equity (as it is a transaction with owners in their capacity as owners) while the gain or loss from the sale of the remaining 51% interest would be recognised in profit or loss (once control is lost). In the second case, however, a gain or loss on the sale of the whole 70% interest would be recognised in profit or loss. However, if an entity wanted to conceal losses on a disposal of a subsidiary, the opportunities are limited given the requirements of IAS 36 and IFRS 5, which usually require recognition of an impairment loss even before the completion of any sale, [IFRS 10.BCZ186], (although they do not require reclassification of losses recognised in OCI). In addition, IFRS 10 has the following guidance: [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B97] Extract from IFRS 10 B97 A parent might lose control of a subsidiary in two or more arrangements (transactions). However, sometimes circumstances indicate that the multiple arrangements should be accounted for as a single transaction. In determining whether to account for the arrangements as a single transaction, a parent shall consider all the terms and conditions of the arrangements and their economic effects. One or more of the following indicate that the parent should account for the multiple arrangements as a single transaction: (a) They are entered into at the same time or in contemplation of each other. (b) They form a single transaction designed to achieve an overall commercial effect. (c) The occurrence of one arrangement is dependent on the occurrence of at least one other arrangement. (d) One arrangement considered on its own is not economically justified, but it is economically justified when considered together with other arrangements. An example is when a disposal of shares is priced below market and is compensated for by a subsequent disposal priced above market. These indicators clarify that arrangements that are part of a package are accounted for as a single transaction. How we see it Judgement is needed in determining whether the circumstances really do support multiple arrangements being accounted as a single transaction since there is a risk that an entity might end up accounting for a transaction that is truly separate as part of the transaction in which the loss of control occurred. IFRS 10 is silent on how an entity accounts for multiple arrangements that are part of a single transaction. Depending on the facts and circumstances, the parent accounts for these transactions as either an advance payment or as an immediate disposal. Advance payment - If the parent does not lose control over the subsidiary and access to the benefits associated with ownership until later steps in the transaction, then it accounts for the first step of the transaction as an advance receipt of consideration and continues to consolidate the subsidiary until the later date. In many cases, the assets and liabilities of the consolidated subsidiary could qualify as a disposal group held for sale under IFRS 5 (see Chapter 5 at section 2.1). Immediate disposal - If the parent loses control and access to benefits associated on the first step of the transaction, then it ceases to consolidate the former subsidiary immediately, recognises a gain or loss on disposal, and accounts for the consideration due in the second step as deferred consideration receivable. Illustration 3-7 below shows a fact pattern where the entity would need to evaluate how to account for transactions that are linked. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 3-7: Step-disposal of a subsidiary (2) A parent initially controls 70% of a subsidiary that has net assets of CU1,000,000 and a foreign currency translation loss that was recognised in OCI and is accumulated within equity of CU100,000. Of this amount, CU30,000 was allocated to NCI, and is included within the NCI of CU300,000. No goodwill was recognised in respect of this subsidiary and the subsidiary is not part of a CGU to which goodwill is allocated. In November 20X0, the parent sells 19% of its ownership interest for CU200,000. In February 20X1, the parent sells the remaining 51% for CU550,000 in an arrangement that is considered part of a single overall transaction. It is assumed that there are no gains or losses in the intervening period. The effects of discounting for the deferral of consideration are assessed as immaterial. The net assets of the subsidiary are not impaired under IAS 36, which is confirmed by the fact that the total sales price exceeds the parent’s share in the net assets by CU50,000 (CU750,000 - CU700,000). The total loss on disposal can be calculated as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Proceeds from the sale **(CU200,000 + CU550,000)** Dr NCI derecognised Dr Loss on disposal of the subsidiary attributable to the parent Cr Net assets of the subsidiary derecognised 1,000 Cr Reclassification of parent’s share of the loss in OCI If the parent is considered not to have lost control over the investment in the subsidiary until February 20X1 then it accounts for the CU200,000 received in the first step of the transaction as an advance receipt of consideration. The parent continues to consolidate the subsidiary until the later date, at which point the loss on disposal of CU20,000 would be recognised. If the parent is considered to have lost control over the investment in the subsidiary on the first step of the transaction, then it ceases to consolidate the former subsidiary immediately, recognises a loss on disposal of CU20,000, and accounts for the consideration of CU550,000 due in the second step as deferred consideration receivable. 3.5 Treatment of OCI on loss of control of a subsidiary If a parent loses control of a subsidiary, all amounts previously recognised in OCI are accounted for on the same basis as would be required if the parent had directly disposed of the related assets or liabilities. If a gain or loss previously recognised in OCI would be reclassified to profit or loss on the disposal of the related assets or liabilities, the parent reclassifies the gain or loss from equity to profit or loss (as a reclassification adjustment) when it loses control of the subsidiary. [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98(c), IFRS 10.B99]. Therefore: (a) If a revaluation surplus previously recognised in OCI would be transferred directly to retained earnings on the disposal of the asset, the parent transfers the revaluation surplus directly to retained earnings when it loses control of the subsidiary. [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B99]. (b) Remeasurement gains or losses on a defined benefit plan recognised in OCI would not be reclassified to profit or loss when the parent loses control of the subsidiary, but may be transferred within equity. [IAS 19.122]. (c) On disposal of a subsidiary that includes a foreign operation, the cumulative amount of the exchange differences relating to that foreign operation (that is recognised in OCI and accumulated in the separate component of equity) is reclassified from equity to profit or loss, except for the amounts that have been attributed to the NCI. Those amounts are derecognised, and not reclassified to profit or loss. [IAS 21.48, IAS 21.48A, IAS 21.48B]. This would appear to mean that it is only the parent’s share of the cumulative exchange differences that is reclassified; those attributable to the NCI are not reclassified as they have already been included within the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised as part of calculating the gain or loss attributable to the parent. (d) Changes in fair value of debt instruments measured at fair value through OCI and accumulated in equity would be reclassified from equity to profit or loss. [IFRS 9.4.1.2A, IFRS 9.5.7.10]. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests (e) The effective portion of gains and losses on hedging instruments in a cash flow or net investment hedge previously recognised in OCI and accumulated in equity would be reclassified from equity to profit or loss. [IFRS 9.6.5.12, IFRS 9.6.5.14]. (f) The ‘cost of hedging’ adjustments previously recognised in OCI and accumulated in equity would be reclassified from equity to profit or loss. [IFRS 9.6.5.15, IFRS 9.6.5.16]. How we see it There are two different interpretations of how to treat OCI accumulated in equity (other than exchange differences related to the net investment in foreign operations) that would be reclassified to profit or loss on the disposal of the related assets or liabilities, both of which are acceptable: • Approach 1 Reclassification of OCI related to parent interest only • Approach 2 Reclassification of OCI related to parent and the NCI These approaches for the treatment of OCI accumulated in equity (other than exchange differences related to the net investment in foreign operations) are described below. Approach 1 Reclassification of OCI related to parent interest only IFRS 10 requires derecognition of the NCI (including any components of OCI attributable to them) at the date when control is lost, which implies derecognition of the NCI without any need for reclassification. [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98(a)]. In addition, IFRS 10 requires recognition of a gain or loss in profit or loss to be attributable to the parent, [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98(d)], which again implies that there is no reclassification of OCI in respect of the NCI. Approach 1 is more consistent with the treatment of exchange differences relating to the net investment in foreign operations, as described at (c) above. Approach 2 Reclassification of OCI related to parent and the NCI IFRS 10 specifically requires that “if a gain or loss previously recognised in other comprehensive income would be reclassified to profit or loss on the disposal of the related assets or liabilities, the parent shall reclassify the gain or loss from equity to profit or loss (as a reclassification adjustment) when it loses control of the subsidiary.” [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98(c), IFRS 10.B99]. That would clearly require reclassification of the entire balance of OCI accumulated within equity. However, where this is done, the portion of the reclassification adjustment attributable to the NCI is included as part of the profit or loss attributable to the NCI, not as part of the profit or loss attributable to the parent. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 3-8 below shows the application of the above requirements. Illustration 3-8: Reclassification of OCI on loss of control of a subsidiary A parent sells a 70% interest in a 90%-owned subsidiary to a third party for a cash consideration of CU28 million. The fair value of the 20% interest retained by the parent is CU8 million. At the date of disposal, the net assets of the subsidiary were CU30 million. Included within those net assets, the subsidiary had recognised, in its own financial statements, the following: • Property, plant and equipment of CU5 million that has resulted in a revaluation reserve of CU2 million • Derivative financial assets of CU3.2 million (designated in a cash flow hedge) that have resulted in a cash flow hedge reserve of CU3 million • A net defined benefit liability of CU3 million that has resulted in a reserve relating to net remeasurement losses of CU1.5 million on the defined benefit plan • Net assets of a foreign operation of CU10 million that has resulted in a cumulative translation reserve in respect of net translation gains on the foreign operation of CU4 million In the parent’s consolidated financial statements, the parent has recognised 90% of these reserves in equivalent equity reserve balances, with the 10% attributable to the NCI included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI. The impact of the subsidiary on the statement of financial position included in the parent’s consolidated financial statements immediately prior to the disposal is as follows: CU m CU m Net assets of the subsidiary 30.00 Equity attributable to parent • PP&E revaluation reserve 1.80 • Cash flow hedge reserve 2.70 • Net remeasurement loss reserve on defined benefit plan (1.35) • Cumulative translation reserve 3.60 • Other equity/retained earnings 20.25 Total equity attributable to parent 27.0 NCI 3.00 If the parent follows Approach 1 for the cash flow hedge reserve and makes a reserve transfer for the net remeasurement loss reserve on the defined benefit plan and the PP&E revaluation reserve, the impact of the disposal on the parent’s consolidated financial statements is as follows: CU m CU m DR CR Cash proceeds from the disposal 28.00 Retained 20% investment at fair value 8.00 Derecognition of net assets of the subsidiary 30.00 Derecognition of NCI 3.00 Reserves reclassified to profit or loss • Cash flow hedge reserve (a) 2.70 • Cumulative translation reserve (b) 3.60 Reserves transferred to retained earnings: • PP&E revaluation reserve (c) 1.80 • Net remeasurement loss reserve on defined benefit plan (d) 1.35 Retained earnings resulting from above transfers 0.45 Gain on disposal (attributable to parent) 15.30 Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests The parent: (a) Reclassifies its CU2.7 million cash flow hedge reserve to profit or loss for the period. This is reflected in the gain on disposal. The remaining 10% (i.e., CU0.3 million) is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised as part of the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. (b) Reclassifies its cumulative translation reserve of CU3.6 million (90% × CU4 million) relating to the parent’s interest to profit or loss. Again, this is reflected in the gain on disposal. The CU0.4 million (10% × CU4 million) relating to the NCI is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised in calculating the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. (c) Transfers its revaluation reserve of CU1.8 million relating to its 90% share of the revaluation surplus on property, plant and equipment within equity to retained earnings. It is not reclassified to profit or loss. The remaining 10% attributable to the NCI is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised in calculating the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. (d) Transfers its reserve of CU1.35 million relating to its 90% share of the net remeasurement losses on the defined benefit plan within equity to retained earnings. It is not reclassified to profit or loss. The remaining 10% attributable to the NCI is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised in calculating the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. This results in the same position as if the parent had not recognised a separate reserve for the net remeasurement losses on the defined benefit liability but had included them within retained earnings. If, instead, the parent follows Approach 2 for the cash flow hedge reserve, the impact of the disposal on the parent’s consolidated financial statements is as follows: CU m CU m DR CR Cash proceeds from the disposal 28.00 Retained 20% investment at fair value 8.00 Derecognition of net assets of the subsidiary 30.00 Derecognition of NCI 3.00 Reserves reclassified to profit or loss: • Cash flow hedge reserve (a) 2.70 • Cumulative translation reserve (b) 3.60 NCI (reclassification of cash flow hedge reserve) (a) 0.30 Reserves transferred to retained earnings: • PP&E revaluation reserve (c) 1.80 • Net remeasurement loss reserve on defined benefit plan (d) 1.35 Retained earnings resulting from above transfers 0.45 Gain on reclassification of cash flow hedge reserve (attributable to NCI) (a) 0.30 Gain on disposal (attributable to parent) 15.30 The parent: (a) Reclassifies the entire CU3 million surplus on the cash flow hedge reserve to profit or loss for the period. The 90% of the balance (i.e., CU2.7 million) attributable to the parent is included within the gain on disposal that is attributable to the parent, while the remaining 10% (i.e., CU0.3 million) attributable to the NCI is reclassified to profit or loss and is included within the profit or loss attributable to the NCI. (b) Reclassifies its cumulative translation reserve of CU3.6 million (90% × CU4 million) relating to the parent’s interest to profit or loss. Again, this is reflected in the gain on disposal. The CU0.4 million (10% × CU4 million) relating to the NCI is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised in calculating the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests (c) Transfers its revaluation reserve of CU1.8 million relating to its 90% share of the revaluation surplus on property, plant and equipment within equity to retained earnings. It is not reclassified to profit or loss. The remaining 10% attributable to the NCI is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised in calculating the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. (d) Transfers its reserve of CU1.35 million relating to its 90% share of the net remeasurement losses on the defined benefit plan within equity to retained earnings. It is not reclassified to profit or loss. The remaining 10% attributable to the NCI is included as part of the carrying amount of the NCI that is derecognised in calculating the gain or loss recognised on disposal of the subsidiary but is not reclassified to profit or loss nor is it transferred within equity. This results in the same position as if the parent had not recognised a separate reserve for the net remeasurement losses on the defined benefit liability but had included them within retained earnings. 3.6 Deemed disposal A subsidiary may cease to be a subsidiary, or a group may reduce its interest in a subsidiary, other than by actual disposal. This is commonly referred to as a ‘deemed disposal’. Deemed disposals may arise for many reasons, including: • A group does not take up its full allocation in a rights issue by a subsidiary in the group • A subsidiary declares scrip dividends that are not taken up by its parent, so that the parent’s proportional interest is diminished • Another party exercises its options or warrants issued by a subsidiary • A subsidiary issues shares to third parties • A contractual agreement by which a group obtained control over a subsidiary is terminated or changed A deemed disposal that results in the loss of control of a subsidiary is accounted for as a regular disposal. This accounting is shown in Illustration 3-9 below. Illustration 3-9: Deemed disposal through share issue by subsidiary A parent entity P owns 600,000 of the 1,000,000 shares issued by its subsidiary S, giving it a 60% interest. The carrying value of S’s net identifiable assets in the consolidated financial statements of P is CU120 million. P measured the NCI using the proportionate share of net assets; therefore, the NCI is CU48 million (40% x CU120 million). In addition, goodwill of CU15 million was recognised upon the original acquisition of S and has not subsequently been impaired. The goodwill is allocated to S for the purposes of impairment testing. Subsequently, S issues 500,000 shares to a new investor for CU80 million. As a result, P’s 600,000 shares now represent 40% of the 1,500,000 shares issued by S in total and S becomes an associate of P. IFRS 10 requires the remaining interest in the former subsidiary to be recognised at fair value. P considers that, based on the requirements of IFRS 13, the fair value of its 600,000 shares in S is CU96 million. This results in a gain of CU9 million on disposal, recognised as follows: CU m CU m Dr Interest in S Dr NCI Cr Gain on disposal Cr Net assets of S (previously consolidated) Cr Goodwill (previously shown separately) As indicated in section 3.3.2.F above, the IASB has discussed issues relating to the unit of account for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates, and their fair value measurement under IFRS 13. 3.7 Demergers and distributions of non-cash assets to owners Groups may dispose of subsidiaries by way of a demerger. This situation typically involves the transfer of the subsidiaries to be disposed of, either directly to shareholders, by way of a dividend in kind or to a newly formed entity in exchange for the issue of shares by that entity to the shareholders of the disposing entity. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests IFRS 10 requires recognition of a distribution of shares of the subsidiary to owners in their capacity as owners but does not describe how to account for such transactions. [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B98(b)]. Instead, IFRIC 17 Distributions of Non-cash Assets to Owners addresses distributions of subsidiary shares to shareholders. [IFRIC 17.9]. The application of IFRIC 17 in the context of demergers is discussed below. The application of IFRIC 17 to assets in general is discussed in Chapter 9 at section 2.4.2. 3.7.1 Scope of IFRIC 17 The scope of IFRIC 17 is explained in the following extract. [IFRIC 17.3]. Extract from IFRIC 17 This Interpretation applies to the following types of non-reciprocal distributions of assets by an entity to its owners acting in their capacity as owners: (a) distributions of non-cash assets (eg items of property, plant and equipment, businesses as defined in IFRS 3, ownership interests in another entity or disposal groups as defined in IFRS 5); and (b) distributions that give owners a choice of receiving either non-cash assets or a cash alternative. See Chapter 10 at section 3.2 for further guidance on the definition of a business in IFRS 3, and Chapter 5 at section 2.1 for further guidance on disposal groups as defined in IFRS 5. The scope of IFRIC 17 is limited in several respects: • It only applies to distributions in which all owners of the same class of equity instruments are treated equally. [IFRIC 17.4]. • It does not apply to “a distribution of a non-cash asset that is ultimately controlled by the same party or parties before and after the distribution”, [IFRIC 17.5], which means that IFRIC 17 does not apply when: • A group of individual shareholders receiving the distribution, as a result of contractual arrangements, collectively have the power to govern financial and operating policies of the entity making the distribution so as to obtain benefits from its activities, [IFRIC 17.6], • An entity distributes some of its ownership interests in a subsidiary but retains control of the subsidiary. The entity making a distribution that results in the entity recognising NCI in its subsidiary accounts for the distribution in accordance with IFRS 10. [IFRIC 17.7]. In this situation, the requirements of IFRS 10 discussed in section 4 below would be applied. This exclusion applies to the separate, individual, and consolidated financial statements of an entity that makes the distribution. [IFRIC 17.5]. • It only addresses the accounting by an entity that makes a non-cash asset distribution. It does not address the accounting by shareholders who receive the distribution. [IFRIC 17.8]. The rest of this section describes the requirements of IFRIC 17. However, as noted above, certain transactions fall out of scope of the Interpretation. Chapter 9 at section 4.4.1.D includes guidance on the accounting by a subsidiary making a distribution of a non-cash asset to its parent. 3.7.2 Recognition and measurement in IFRIC 17 An entity making a non-cash distribution to its owners recognises a liability to pay a dividend when the dividend is appropriately authorised and is no longer at the discretion of the entity. This is the date: [IFRIC 17.10] • When declaration of the dividend (e.g., by management or the board of directors) is approved by the relevant authority (e.g., shareholders) if the jurisdiction requires such approval Or • When the dividend is declared (e.g., by management or the board of directors) if the jurisdiction does not require further approval An entity measures the liability at the fair value of the assets to be distributed. [IFRIC 17.11]. If the owners have a choice between receiving a non-cash asset or cash, the entity estimates the dividend payable by considering both the fair value of each alternative and the associated probability of owners selecting each alternative. [IFRIC 17.12]. IFRIC 17 does not specify any method of assessing probability nor its effect on measurement. In a demerger involving the distribution of shares in a subsidiary, the fair value will be determined based on the guidance in IFRS 13. As indicated in section 3.3.2.F above, the IASB has discussed issues relating to the unit of account for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates, and their fair value measurement under IFRS 13. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests IFRS 5’s requirements apply also to a non-current asset (or disposal group) that is classified as held for distribution to owners acting in their capacity as owners (held for distribution to owners). [IFRS 5.5A, IFRS 5.12A]. This means that a non-current asset (or disposal group) classified as held for distribution within scope of IFRS 5 will be carried at the lower of its carrying amount and fair value less costs to distribute (i.e., incremental costs directly attributable to the distribution, excluding finance costs and income tax expense). [IFRS 5.15A]. Assets not subject to the measurement provisions of IFRS 5 are measured in accordance with the relevant standard. [IFRS 5.2, IFRS 5.5]. See further discussion in Chapter 5 at section 2.2. At the end of each reporting period and at the date of settlement, the carrying amount of the dividend payable is adjusted to reflect any changes in the fair value of the assets being distributed and changes are recognised in equity as adjustments to the amount of the distribution. [IFRIC 17.11, IFRIC 17.13]. When the dividend payable is settled, any difference between the carrying amount of the assets distributed and the carrying amount of the dividend payable is recognised as a separate line item in profit or loss. [IFRIC 17.14, IFRIC 17.15]. IFRIC 17 does not express any preference for particular line items or captions in the income statement. The non-cash assets that are to be distributed are measured in accordance with other applicable IFRS accounting standards up to the time of settlement as IFRIC 17 does not override the recognition and measurement requirements of other IFRS accounting standards. While the IFRS IC recognised concerns about the potential ‘accounting mismatch’ in equity resulting from measuring the dividend payable and the related assets on a different basis, [IFRIC 17.BC55], it concluded that: “… there was no support in IFRSs for requiring a remeasurement of the assets because of a decision to distribute them. The IFRIC noted that the mismatch concerned arises only with respect to assets that are not carried at fair value already. The IFRIC also noted that the accounting mismatch is the inevitable consequence of IFRSs using different measurement attributes at different times with different triggers for the remeasurement of different assets and liabilities.” [IFRIC 17.BC56]. 3.7.3 Presentation and disclosure An entity is required to disclose the following information in respect of distributions of non-cash assets within the scope of IFRIC 17: • The carrying amount of the dividend payable at the beginning and end of the reporting period [IFRIC 17.16] • The increase or decrease in the carrying amount recognised in the reporting period as result of a change in the fair value of the assets to be distributed [IFRIC 17.16] • If an entity declares a dividend to distribute a non-cash asset after the end of a reporting period but before the financial statements are authorised for issue: [IFRIC 17.17] • The nature of the asset to be distributed • The carrying amount of the asset to be distributed as of the end of the reporting period • The fair value of the asset to be distributed as of the end of the reporting period, if it is different from its carrying amount • Information about the method(s) used to determine that fair value required by IFRS 13.93(b), (d), (g) and (i) and IFRS 13.99 (see Chapter 15 at sections 19.1 and 19.3) [IFRS 13.93, IFRS 13.99] # Chapter 5 at section 3 discusses the presentation requirements of IFRS 5 where the demerger meets the definition of a discontinued operation. Chapter 5 at section 2.2.4 discusses the presentation of non-current assets and disposal groups held for sale. The same requirements apply to non-current assets and disposal groups held for distribution. [IFRS 5.5A]. Less extensive disclosures are needed where IFRS 19 is applied (see Chapter 4 at section 7).26 **[IFRS 19.38-40, IFRS19.95-97].** IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures was issued by the IASB in May 2024 to simplify the preparation of financial statements by eligible subsidiaries. Eligible subsidiaries that elect to apply IFRS 19 are allowed to provide reduced disclosures while still applying the recognition, measurement and presentation requirements in other IFRS accounting standards. IFRS 19 is effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027, with earlier application permitted (subject to jurisdictional endorsement). See Chapter 4 at section 7 for further details on the requirements of IFRS 19. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Changes in ownership interest without a loss of control Extract from IFRS 10 Changes in a parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary that do not result in the parent losing control of the subsidiary are equity transactions (ie transactions with owners in their capacity as owners). A parent’s ownership interest can change without a loss of control, e.g., when a parent buys shares from or sells shares to NCI, a subsidiary redeems shares held by NCI (and changes the NCI’s proportionate interest), or when a subsidiary issues new shares to NCI (and changes the NCI’s proportionate interest). Extract from IFRS 10 Changes in the proportion held by non-controlling interests B96 When the proportion of the equity held by non-controlling interests changes, an entity shall adjust the carrying amounts of the controlling and non-controlling interests to reflect the changes in their relative interests in the subsidiary. The entity shall recognise directly in equity any difference between the amount by which the non-controlling interests are adjusted and the fair value of the consideration paid or received, and attribute it to the owners of the parent. This means that no changes to a subsidiary’s assets (including goodwill) and liabilities are recognised in a transaction in which a parent increases or decreases its ownership interest in a subsidiary but retains control. [IFRS 10.BCZ173]. Increases or decreases in the ownership interest in a subsidiary do not result in the recognition of a gain or loss but adjust the carrying amounts of the controlling interest and NCI in equity. **[IFRS 10.23, IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B96].** IFRS 12 requires an entity to disclose information on the consequences of such changes in ownership interest, through the presentation of a schedule showing the effects on the equity attributable to the owners of the parent (i.e., parent equity) of any changes in its ownership interest in a subsidiary that do not result in a loss of control. [IFRS 12.10(b)(iii), IFRS 12.18]. See Chapter 14 at section 4.5.1. 4.1 Reattribution of OCI If there has been a partial disposal of a subsidiary without a loss of control and the disposal includes a foreign operation, the proportionate share of the cumulative amount of exchange differences recognised in OCI is reattributed to the NCI in that foreign operation (see Chapter 16 at section 6.6.2.B). [IAS 21.48C]. In other words, these exchange differences are not reclassified to profit or loss. The accounting is shown in Illustration 4-1 below. If the entity subsequently disposes of the remainder of its interest in the subsidiary, the exchange differences previously attributed to the NCI (including prior reattributions on changes of ownership without loss of control) are derecognised (i.e., along with the rest of the NCI balance) but are not separately reclassified to profit or loss (see Chapter 16 at section 6.6.1). [IAS 21.48B]. In other words, on loss of control, only the exchange differences attributable to the controlling interest immediately before loss of control are reclassified to profit or loss. [IAS 21.48]. The accounting is shown in Illustration 4-3 below and Illustration 6-10 in Chapter 16 at section 6.6.1.A. Although not explicitly addressed in IFRS 10 or IAS 21, the IASB has clarified that IFRS accounting standards require the reattribution of other amounts recognised in OCI. The May 2009 IASB Update noted that in the Board’s view “there is no need to clarify the following points, because the relevant requirements are clear: “ª … “ª When a change in ownership in a subsidiary occurs but does not result in the loss of control, the parent must reattribute other comprehensive income between the owners of the parent and the non- controlling interest.”27 *Reference: IASB Update, May 2009, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 4-1 below shows the accounting for reattribution of OCI. Illustration 4-1: Reattribution of OCI upon a decrease in ownership interest that does not result in a loss of control A parent has a wholly-owned subsidiary that has net assets of CU4,000,000, and total OCI accumulated within equity of CU1,000,000 related to exchange gains on a foreign operation. No goodwill has been recognised in respect of this subsidiary. The parent sells a 10% interest in the subsidiary for CU500,000 and does not lose control. The carrying amount of the NCI is CU400,000 which includes CU100,000 (i.e., 10%) of the total OCI of CU1,000,000 related to exchange differences on a foreign operation reattributed to the NCI (as shown below). The parent accounts for the transaction through equity as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Cash Dr Parent’s share of OCI **(CU1,000,000 × 10%)** Cr Other components of parent equity Cr NCI’s share of OCI **(CU1,000,000 × 10%)** Cr NCI (excluding share of OCI) **((CU4,000,000 × 10%) -** **(CU1,000,000 × 10%))** The effect of the above journals is an increase in the carrying amount of NCI of CU400,000, a reduction in the parent’s cumulative translation reserve from CU1,000,000 to CU900,000, and an increase in other components of parent equity of CU200,000. As IFRS accounting standards do not specify where the adjustment to parent equity is recognised, this would be permitted in retained earnings or another component of parent equity that will not be reclassified on loss of control. Illustration 4-2 below shows the reattribution approach upon an increase in ownership interest. Illustration 4-2: Reattribution of OCI upon an increase in ownership interest that does not result in a loss of control A parent holds an 80% interest in a subsidiary that has net assets of CU4,000,000. No goodwill has been recognised in respect of this subsidiary. The carrying amount of the 20% NCI is CU800,000 which includes CU200,000 that represents the NCI’s share of total OCI of CU1,000,000 related to exchange gains on a foreign operation. The parent acquires an additional 10% interest in the subsidiary for CU500,000, which increases its total interest to 90%. The carrying amount of the NCI is now CU400,000 which includes CU100,000 (i.e., 10%) of the total OCI of CU1,000,000 related to exchange differences on a foreign operation, after reattributing CU100,000 to the parent (as shown below). The parent accounts for the transaction through equity as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr NCI’s share of OCI (CU1,000,000 × 10%) Dr NCI (excluding share of OCI) **((CU800,000 × 10% ÷ 20%) -** **(CU1,000,000 × 10%))** Dr Other components of parent’s equity Cr Parent’s share of OCI **(CU1,000,000 × 10%)** Cr Cash The effect of the above journals is a reduction in the carrying amount of NCI of CU400,000, an increase in the parent’s cumulative translation reserve from CU800,000 to CU900,000, and a decrease in other components of parent equity of CU200,000. As IFRS accounting standards do not specify where the adjustment to parent equity is recognised, this would be permitted in retained earnings or another component of parent equity that will not be reclassified on loss of control. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 4-3 below shows the reclassification of reattributed exchange differences upon subsequent loss of control. This demonstrates that the reattribution of the exchange differences arising on the change in the parent’s ownership of the subsidiary (as shown in Illustration 4-1 and Illustration 4-2 above) affects the gain recognised on loss of control of the subsidiary. Illustration 4-3: Reclassification of reattributed exchange differences upon subsequent loss of control Assume the same facts as in Illustration 4-1 or Illustration 4-2 above. Following those transactions, the parent now holds a 90% interest in a subsidiary that has net assets of CU4,000,000. No goodwill has been recognised in respect of this subsidiary. The carrying amount of the 10% NCI is CU400,000 which includes CU100,000 of the total OCI of CU1,000,000 related to exchange gains on a foreign operation, as reattributed to the NCI. The parent subsequently sells its 90% interest for CU4,700,000. For the purposes of illustration, there have been no subsequent changes in net assets nor OCI up to the date of sale. The parent accounts for the transaction as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Cash proceeds from sale 4,700 Dr NCI derecognised Dr Parent’s share of OCI reclassified **(CU1,000,000 × 90%)** Cr Net assets of subsidiary derecognised 4,000 Cr Gain recognised on disposal of subsidiary attributable to parent 2,000 4.2 Goodwill attributable to NCI It is not clear under IFRS 10 what happens to the NCI’s share of goodwill, when accounting for transactions with NCI. How we see it We believe that when there are changes in their relative ownership interests, the parent is required to reallocate a proportion of the goodwill between the controlling interest and NCI. However, the amount of goodwill, if any, to be allocated may depend on whether NCI was recognised at fair value or not on initial acquisition, and on whether there is an increase or decrease in ownership of the NCI without a loss on control. If an appropriate amount of goodwill is not reallocated, the loss recognised upon loss of control (see section 3.2 above), or goodwill impairment would not reflect the ownership interest in the subsidiary applicable to that NCI (see Chapter 21 at section 9). The issues arising include: • Calculation of the ‘gross up’ of the carrying amount of goodwill (for the purposes of the impairment test) because the NCI is measured at its proportionate share of net identifiable assets and hence its share of goodwill is not recognised • The allocation of impairment losses between the parent and NCI • Reallocation of goodwill between NCI and the controlling interest after a change in a parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary that does not result in loss of control Under IFRS 3, the proportion of goodwill that is attributable to NCI is not necessarily equal to the ownership percentage. The most common situation where this occurs is when the parent recognised the NCI at its proportionate share of the acquiree’s identifiable net assets and therefore does not recognise any goodwill for the NCI (see Chapter 10 at sections 5.1 and 8.2). This situation might also occur because goodwill has been recognised for both the parent’s interest and the NCI, but the parent’s goodwill reflects a control premium that was paid upon acquisition (see Chapter 10 at section 8.1). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 4-4 below shows one approach to reallocating goodwill where there is a change in ownership of the subsidiary with no loss of control (for a situation where the NCI is recognised initially at its fair value). Illustration 4-4: Reallocation of goodwill to NCI A parent pays CU920 million to acquire an 80% interest in a subsidiary that owns net assets with a fair value of CU1,000 million. NCI is recognised initially at its fair value of CU220 million at the acquisition date. Share of net assets Share of goodwill Total CU m CU m CU m Parent NCI 1,000 1,140 Decrease in ownership percentage A year after the acquisition, the parent sells a 20% interest in the subsidiary to a third party for CU265 million. There has been no change in the net assets of the subsidiary since acquisition. The parent’s interest decreases to 60% and its share of net assets decreases to CU600 million. Correspondingly, the share of net assets attributable to the NCI increases from CU200 million to CU400 million. The parent company sold a 20% interest in its subsidiary. Therefore, one approach for reallocating goodwill is to allocate CU30 million (20% ÷ 80% × CU120 million) of the parent’s goodwill to the NCI. After the transaction, the parent’s share of goodwill is CU90 million (CU120 million - CU30 million). In its consolidated financial statements, the parent accounts for this transaction as follows: CU m CU m Dr Cash Cr NCI ((CU400m - CU200m) + CU30m) Cr Equity of the parent Increase in ownership percentage Taking the initial fact pattern as a starting point, the parent acquires an additional 10% interest in the subsidiary for CU115 million. There has been no change in the net assets of the subsidiary since acquisition. The parent’s interest increases to 90% and its share of net assets increases to CU900 million. Correspondingly, the share of net assets attributable to NCI is reduced from CU200 million to CU100 million. The parent acquired half of the NCI. Using the proportionate allocation approach discussed above, the parent allocates CU10 million (10% ÷ 20% × CU20 million) of the NCI’s goodwill to the parent. In its consolidated financial statements, the parent accounts for this transaction as follows: CU m CU m Dr NCI ((CU200m - CU100m) + CU10m) Dr Equity of the parent Cr Cash In Illustration 4-4 above, the NCI was recognised and measured at its fair value at the acquisition date. If the NCI had been measured based on its proportionate share of net assets, the proportionate allocation approach described in the Illustration would have resulted in the same accounting for the transaction where the parent’s ownership interest had decreased. However, where the parent increased its ownership interest, as the carrying amount of the NCI did not include any amount for goodwill, the adjustment to the NCI would only have been CU100 million resulting in a debit to the parent’s equity of CU15 million. How we see it Reallocation of the goodwill between parent and NCI on a proportionate basis is one method that may result in relevant and reliable information. However, other approaches may also be appropriate depending on the circumstances (see Chapter 21 at section 9.3). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests We consider that an entity is not precluded from attributing goodwill on a basis other than ownership percentages if to do so is reasonable, e.g., because the NCI is measured on a proportionate share (rather than fair value) and because of the existence of a control premium. In such circumstances, an allocation approach which takes into account the acquirer’s control premium will result in a goodwill balance that most closely resembles the balance that would have been recorded had the NCI been recorded at fair value. An entity may also be able to allocate impairment losses on a basis that recognises the disproportionate sharing of the controlling interest and the NCI in the goodwill book value. This is discussed further in # Chapter 21 at section 9 which addresses how an entity tests goodwill for impairment where there is NCI. 4.3 Non-cash acquisition of NCI One issue considered by the IFRS IC is the accounting for the purchase of NCI by the controlling shareholder when the consideration includes non-cash items, such as an item of property, plant and equipment. More specifically, the submitter asked the IFRS IC to clarify whether the difference between the fair value of the consideration given and the carrying amount of such consideration should be recognised in equity or in profit or loss. The submitter asserted that, according to the requirements of the then IAS 27 Consolidated and Separate Financial Statements (now reflected in IFRS 10), the difference described should be recognised in equity, whereas applying IFRIC 17 by analogy, the difference should be recognised in profit or loss (see section 3.7 above). The IFRS IC noted that the requirements of the then IAS 27 (now reflected in IFRS 10), deal solely with the difference between the carrying amount of the NCI and the fair value of the consideration given; this difference is required to be recognised in equity. These requirements do not deal with the difference between the fair value of the consideration given and the carrying amount of such consideration. [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B96]. The difference between the fair value of the assets transferred and their carrying amount arises from the derecognition of those assets. Since IFRS accounting standards generally require an entity to recognise, in profit or loss, any gain or loss arising from the derecognition of an asset,28 this difference has to be recognised in profit or loss. 4.4 Transaction costs Although IFRS 10 is clear that changes in a parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary that do not result in the parent losing control of the subsidiary are equity transactions (i.e., transactions with owners in their capacity as owners), [IFRS 10.23], it does not specifically address how to account for related transaction costs. Only incremental costs directly attributable to the equity transaction that otherwise would have been avoided qualify as transaction costs. [IAS 32.37]. How we see it In our view, any incremental directly attributable transaction costs incurred to acquire an outstanding NCI in a subsidiary, or to sell an NCI in a subsidiary without loss of control, are deducted from equity. This is regardless of whether the consideration is in cash or shares. This is consistent with both guidance elsewhere in IFRS accounting standards regarding the treatment of such costs, [IAS 32.35, IAS 32.37, IAS 1.109], and the view expressed by the IFRS IC.29 Although where shares are given as consideration there is no change in total consolidated equity, there are in fact two transactions - an issue of new equity and a repurchase of existing equity. The entity accounts for the transaction costs on the two elements in the same manner as if they had occurred separately. The tax effects of transaction costs of equity instruments are discussed in Chapter 29 at section 10.3.5. IFRS accounting standards do not specify where to allocate the costs in equity - in particular, whether to the parent (who incurred the costs) or to the NCI (whose equity was issued/repurchased). IFRIC Update, January 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, May 2009, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests How we see it As IFRS accounting standards have no guidance, the parent determines where in equity to allocate the transaction costs incurred to acquire or sell an outstanding NCI, based on the facts and circumstances surrounding the change in ownership, and any legal requirements of the jurisdiction. Regardless of the account in equity to which the charge is allocated, we believe that the amount is not reclassified to profit or loss in future periods. Consequently, if the costs are allocated to NCI, this amount must be separately tracked. If a subsidiary is later sold in a separate transaction (i.e., loss of control), the transaction costs previously recognised directly in equity to acquire or sell NCI are not reclassified from equity to profit and loss, because they do not represent components of OCI. The need to track any costs allocated to NCI (if material) is explained in the following Illustration. Illustration 4-5: Treatment of transaction costs arising on a previous NCI purchase/sale on subsequent loss of control of a subsidiary Parent P owned 80% of the shares in Subsidiary S since its establishment (so there is no goodwill). P subsequently purchased 10% of the minority investor’s shares for CU150,000, incurring transaction costs of CU10,000. At the time of the transaction, the net assets of S included in the consolidation were CU1m. Two years later, the net assets of S had increased to CU2m and P sold its 90% ownership interest in S for CU3m. There were no amounts in OCI required to be reclassified on the loss of control of S. If the transaction costs were originally debited to parent equity, the gain on disposal would be CU1.2m (i.e., CU3m proceeds less CU2m net assets of S included in the consolidation plus CU0.2m derecognition of NCI). If the transaction costs were originally debited to NCI, the gain on disposal (if no re-allocation of the transaction costs was made at the date of disposal), would be CU1.19m (i.e., CU3m proceeds less CU2m net assets of S included in the consolidation plus CU0.19m derecognition of NCI). This is a lower gain and is the same amount that would be calculated if the transaction costs had been debited to parent equity and recycled to profit or loss on the disposal. Since the transaction costs are not a component of OCI, this would be inappropriate and, consequently, a transfer would be needed at the date of disposal to reallocate the transaction costs from NCI to parent equity before determining the gain or loss from the loss of control. The same situation would arise if the transaction costs debited to NCI had arisen on an earlier sale of NCI without loss of control. 4.5 Contingent consideration on purchase of NCI IFRS 10 does not provide guidance on accounting for remeasurement of contingent consideration relating to the purchase of NCI. The question arises as to whether the remeasurement is accounted for in profit or loss, or in equity (on the grounds that it is related to the purchase of the NCI - see section 4 above). This discussion assumes that the purchase of NCI is separate from the business combination.
