It is not uncommon for a written put and/or a purchased call option over NCI to be put in place at the time of a business combination. The accounting for such transactions (including forward contracts to sell NCI) is discussed in section 6 below. IFRS 3 addresses the accounting for remeasurement of contingent consideration recognised by the acquirer in a business combination. Contingent consideration is initially recognised at its fair value. Contingent consideration classified as equity is not remeasured and its subsequent settlement is accounted for within equity. Other contingent consideration (not classified as equity) is measured at fair value at each reporting date and changes in its fair value are recognised in profit or loss. This is the case whether or not the contingent consideration is within the scope of IFRS 9. [IFRS 3.39, IFRS 3.40, IFRS 3.58, IFRS 9.4.2.1(e)]. See # Chapter 10 at section 7.1.2. The purchase of NCI is not a business combination and, consequently, IFRS 3’s requirements do not apply. In practice, most contingent consideration meets the definition of a financial liability and would fall within the scope of IFRS 9. How we see it In our view, a distinction needs to be drawn between the initial recognition of the financial liability for contingent consideration arising on the purchase of NCI (which is a transaction with NCI to be reflected as an equity transaction) and its subsequent measurement (which is not a transaction with NCI). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests On initial recognition, contingent consideration is recognised at its fair value, [IFRS 3.40], the NCI purchased is derecognised (and any balancing adjustment is reflected in parent equity). Subsequent movements in contingent consideration classified as a financial liability are recognised in accordance with IFRS 9 (in profit or loss, as explained below) rather than regarded as relating to a transaction with NCI (and recognised in equity). If the contingent consideration is classified as an equity instrument, it will not be remeasured. [IFRS 3.58]. Depending on the terms and conditions, the contingent consideration is classified as a financial liability at amortised cost, or at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.4.2.1, IFRS 9.4.2.2]. In both cases, subsequent remeasurements for any changes in estimated contractual cash flows or fair value, or on final settlement of the financial liability are recognised in profit or loss (except for any fair value movements attributable to own credit risk required to be recognised in OCI, where the financial liability is designated at fair value through profit or loss). See Chapter 44 at sections 2, 3 and 6, and Chapter 46 at sections 2.2, 2.4 and 3. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests NCI 5.1 The definition of NCI Extract from IFRS 10 Appendix A Defined terms non-controlling interest Equity in a subsidiary not attributable, directly or indirectly, to a parent. [IFRS 10 Appendix A]. The principle underlying accounting for NCI is that all residual economic interest holders of any part of the consolidated entity have an equity interest in that consolidated entity. Consequently, NCI relates to consolidated subsidiaries, and not to those investments in subsidiaries accounted for at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 by an investment entity (see # Chapter 7 at section 10.3). [IFRS 10.31, IFRS 10.32, IFRS 10.33]. This principle applies regardless of the decision-making ability of that interest holder and where in the group that interest is held. Therefore, any equity instruments issued by a subsidiary that are not owned by the parent (apart from those that are required to be classified by IAS 32 as financial liabilities in the consolidated financial statements, as discussed in section 5.6 below) are NCI, including, for example: • Ordinary shares • Equity components of convertible debt and other compound financial instruments • Preference shares that are classified as equity (including both those with, and without, an entitlement to a pro rata share of net assets on liquidation) • Warrants that are classified as equity • Options over own shares that are classified as equity • Awards under equity-settled share-based payment transactions Options and warrants classified as equity are NCI, regardless of whether they are vested and regardless of the exercise price (e.g., whether they are ‘in-the-money’ or not). IAS 32 defines an equity instrument as “any contract that evidences a residual interest in the assets of an entity after deducting all of its liabilities”. [IAS 32.11]. This is consistent with the definition of ‘equity’ in the *Reference: IASB’s Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting published in 2018. [CF 4.63, CF 4.64]. Hence, the* reference to ‘equity’ in the definition of NCI refers to those ‘equity instruments’ of a subsidiary that are not attributable, directly or indirectly, to its parent. This also means that financial instruments that are not classified within equity in accordance with IAS 32 (e.g., total return swaps) are not included within the definition of NCI. If a parent has an indirect ownership interest in the subsidiary, this is taken into account in computing the NCI. For example, if a parent has a 60% direct interest in Subsidiary A (which in turn owns 80% of Subsidiary B), there will be a 52% (i.e., 100% - (60% × 80%)) NCI in Subsidiary B’s equity. Where Subsidiary A acquired Subsidiary B in a business combination, there will also be a 40% NCI in the goodwill arising in the consolidated financial statements. This situation is shown in Illustration 5-3 in section 5.2.1 below (note though that, in this specific Illustration, the NCI in the goodwill arising on acquisition of the Target is not simply 40% of the goodwill amount, as the Target has also issued warrants). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 5.2 Initial measurement of NCI 5.2.1 Initial measurement of NCI in a business combination IFRS 3 requires any NCI in an acquiree to be recognised, [IFRS 3.10], but there are differing measurement requirements depending on the type of equity instrument. There is a choice of two measurement methods for those components of NCI that are both present ownership interests and entitle their holders to a proportionate share of the entity’s net assets in the event of a liquidation (referred to here as ‘qualifying NCI’). They can be measured at either: • Option 1 Acquisition-date fair value (consistent with the measurement principle for other components of the business combination) Or • Option 2 The present ownership instruments’ proportionate share in the recognised amounts of the acquiree’s identifiable net assets The choice of method is to be made for each business combination on a transaction-by-transaction basis, rather than being a policy choice. This choice of measurement is discussed in Chapter 10 at section 8. However, this choice is not available for all other components of NCI, which are required to be measured at their acquisition-date fair values, unless another measurement basis is required by IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 3.19]. Of the items listed in section 5.1 above, entities are only given a choice of either proportionate share in the recognised amounts of the acquiree’s identifiable net assets or acquisition-date fair value, for ordinary shares or preference shares classified as equity that are entitled to a pro rata share of net assets on liquidation. Another measurement basis (referred to as a ‘market-based measure’) is required by IFRS 3 for share-based payment transactions classified as equity in accordance with IFRS 2 - these are measured in accordance with the method in IFRS 2. [IFRS 3.30, IFRS 3.B62A, IFRS 3.B62B]. The accounting for replaced and not replaced share- based payment transactions in a business combination is discussed in Chapter 10 at sections 7.2, 8.4 and 11.2.2 and in Chapter 30 at sections 11.2 and 11.3. The other items listed in section 5.1 above, e.g., the equity component of convertible debt or other compound financial instruments, preference shares classified as equity without an entitlement to a pro rata share of net assets upon liquidation, warrants and options over own shares, must be measured at acquisition-date fair value. The initial recognition of NCI in a business combination is discussed in more detail in Chapter 10 at section 8. That chapter also addresses many of the topics discussed in this chapter as well as an indirect acquisition of NCI where the acquiree in a business combination is the owner of an NCI in an acquirer’s existing subsidiary (see Chapter 10 at section 11.5). How we see it While IFRS 3 permits the measurement of ‘qualifying NCI’ (as described above) at either acquisition-date fair value (Option 1) or at the present ownership instruments’ proportionate share in the recognised amounts of the acquiree’s identifiable net assets (Option 2), the standard is not clear on how Option 2 is to be applied where there are other elements of NCI arising in the business combination that are reported at fair value. Sometimes, a non-wholly-owned subsidiary of the group acquires a subsidiary with NCI in a business combination. In this case, we believe that the goodwill arising will be the same as if the parent company itself acquired the subsidiary but that some of the goodwill is attributable to the NCI in the acquiring subsidiary. This is because the respective NCI have an ownership interest in the party making the acquisition that gives rise to the goodwill (and identifiable net assets of the subsidiary). These scenarios are explained further in Illustrations 5-1 to 5-3 below. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 5-1 below is an example showing the measurement of NCI in a business combination. Illustration 5-1: Initial measurement of NCI in a business combination (1) Parent acquires 80% of the ordinary shares of Target for CU950,000 in cash. The total fair value of the equity instruments issued by Target is CU1,165,000 and the fair value of its identifiable net assets is CU850,000. The fair value of the 20% of the ordinary shares owned by non-controlling shareholders is CU190,000. In addition, the subsidiary has also written gross settled call options over its own shares with a fair value of CU25,000, which are considered equity instruments under IAS 32. Option 1 - NCI at fair value The impact of the business combination and the measurement of NCI are as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Fair value of identifiable net assets Dr Goodwill (CU950,000 + CU215,000 - **CU850,000)** Cr Cash Cr **NCI (CU190,000 + CU25,000)** The NCI are measured at the fair value of all equity instruments issued by Target that are not owned by the parent (i.e., ordinary shares and gross settled call options). Option 2 - Qualifying NCI are measured at proportionate share of identifiable net assets The impact of the business combination and the measurement of NCI are as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Fair value of identifiable net assets Dr Goodwill (CU950,000 - (80% × CU850,000) **+ CU25,000)** Cr Cash Cr **NCI (20% × CU850,000 + CU25,000)** The NCI that are present ownership interests and entitle their holders to a proportionate share of the Target’s net assets in the event of liquidation (i.e., the ordinary shares) are measured at the NCI’s proportionate share of the identifiable net assets of Target. The NCI that are not present ownership interests or do not entitle their holders to a proportionate share of the Target’s net assets in the event of liquidation (i.e., the gross settled call options) are measured at their fair value. Reconciliation of goodwill Goodwill as determined under the two methods can be reconciled as follows: CU’000 Option 2: Goodwill (CU950,000 - (80% × CU850,000) + CU25,000) Goodwill related to the NCI in ordinary shares (CU190,000 - 20% × CU850,000) Option 1: Goodwill (CU1,165,000 - CU850,000) This makes clear that Option 2 effectively ignores the goodwill related to ordinary shares that are held by the NCI. In Illustration 5-1 above, under Option 2, the computation of the NCI represented by the ordinary shares was based solely on the fair value of the identifiable net assets, i.e., no deduction was made in respect of the other component of NCI. IFRS 3 does not state whether this is the case. An alternative view would be that such other components of NCI are deducted from the value of the net identifiable net assets acquired based on their acquisition-date fair value (or market-based measure) or based on their liquidation rights, as demonstrated in Illustration 5-2 below. See also Chapter 10 at section 8.3. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 5-2: Initial measurement of NCI in a business combination (2) Option 3 - Qualifying NCI are measured at proportionate share of identifiable net assets net of other components of NCI The impact of the business combination and the measurement of NCI are as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Fair value of identifiable net assets Dr Goodwill (CU950,000 - 80% × (CU850,000 - **CU25,000))** Cr Cash Cr **NCI ((20% × (CU850,000 - CU25,000))** **+ CU25,000)** The difference between goodwill of CU295,000 (Option 2 in Illustration 5-1 above) and CU290,000 is 20% of CU25,000, i.e., the amount attributable to the NCI in the call options. In Illustration 5-1 above, Option 2 resulted in no goodwill being attributable to NCI. However, if the Target had been acquired, not by Parent, but by a 60% owned subsidiary of Parent, we believe that the goodwill recognised remains the same, but that some of the goodwill is attributable to the NCI in the acquiring subsidiary as demonstrated in Illustration 5-3 below. In such circumstances, particular care would be needed in determining the ‘gross up’ of the carrying amount of goodwill (for the purposes of the impairment test), in the allocation of any goodwill impairment to Parent and the NCI, and in any reallocation of goodwill between Parent and the NCI in a transaction with no loss of control. These issues are further discussed in section 4.2 above. Illustration 5-3: Initial measurement of NCI in a business combination by a partly owned subsidiary Assume the same facts as in Illustration 5-1 above, except that a 60% Subsidiary of Parent acquires 80% of the ordinary shares of Target for CU950,000 in cash. Under Option 1, the impact of the business combination and the measurement of NCI in Parent’s consolidated financial statements are as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Fair value of identifiable net assets Dr Goodwill (CU950,000 + CU215,000 - **CU850,000)** Cr Cash Cr NCI in Target (CU190,000 + CU25,000) Cr NCI in 60% Subsidiary - Although the overall impact on the NCI in the 60% Subsidiary is nil, this is represented by: CU’000 CU’000 Dr NCI share of cash consideration (40% × **CU950,000)** Cr NCI share of fair value of identifiable net assets (40% × 80% × CU850,000) Cr NCI share of goodwill (40% × (CU315,000 - **CU25,000 - CU20,000))** The NCI share of goodwill excludes the CU25,000 represented by the warrants and the Target NCI’s interest in the goodwill of CU20,000, recognised by the 60% subsidiary. This is because these amounts are reflected directly in the NCI in Target. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Under Option 2, the impact of the business combination and the measurement of NCI in Parent’s consolidated financial statements are as follows: CU’000 CU’000 Dr Fair value of identifiable net assets Dr Goodwill (CU950,000 - (80% × CU850,000) + **CU25,000)** Cr Cash Cr NCI in Target (20% × CU850,000 + **CU25,000)** Cr NCI in 60% Subsidiary - Although the overall impact on the NCI in the 60% Subsidiary is nil, this is represented by: CU’000 CU’000 Dr NCI share of cash consideration (40% × **CU950,000)** Cr NCI share of fair value of identifiable net assets (40% × 80% × CU850,000) Cr NCI share of goodwill (40% × (CU295,000 - **CU25,000))** 5.2.2 Initial measurement of NCI in a subsidiary that is not a business combination The initial measurement of NCI in a subsidiary that is not a business combination is discussed in sections 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 above. 5.3 Measurement of NCI where an associate holds an interest in a subsidiary of the group Neither IFRS 10 nor IAS 28 explains how to account for NCI when the group owns an associate which has a holding in a subsidiary of the group. NCI is defined as the equity in a subsidiary not attributable, directly or indirectly to a parent. [IFRS 10 Appendix A]. It is unclear whether this is to be computed based on the ownership interests held by the group (i.e., by the parent and any consolidated subsidiary), or whether it also must take into account the indirect ownership of the subsidiary held by the associate. The reciprocal interests can also give rise to a measure of double counting of profits and net assets between the investor and its associate. How we see it Where an associate holds an interest in a subsidiary, we believe that there are two possible approaches to determine the amount of NCI in the subsidiary: • The NCI are determined after considering the associate’s ownership of the subsidiary (‘look through approach’) Or • The NCI are determined based on the holdings of the group in the subsidiary (‘black box approach’) An entity must apply the chosen approach consistently. In applying the ‘look through approach’, the parent must not recognise the share of the subsidiary’s results recognised by the associate applying the equity method, in order to avoid double counting. The ‘black box approach’ will often lead to reporting higher consolidated net assets and results than when using the ‘look through approach’ as this adjustment is not made, although the amounts attributed to the owners of the parent are expected to be the same under both approaches. The two approaches are shown in Illustration 5-4 below. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 5-4: Measurement of NCI where an associate accounted using the equity method holds an interest in a subsidiary Parent P owns directly 60% of subsidiary S. P also directly owns 40% of associate A that in turn holds a 30% interest in S. S has earned profits of CU1,000,000 in the period and has net assets of CU3,000,000 (which are consolidated in both P’s consolidated financial statements and equity accounted by A) at the reporting date. A has net assets of CU1,100,000 (excluding its share of the net assets of S). There are no transactions between A and S (which was not acquired in a business combination). Approach 1 (‘look through approach’) This approach involves determining the ownership attributable to the NCI after considering A’s ownership of S. This approach views the equity method as primarily a method of consolidation (albeit on a single line), as further discussed in Chapter 12 at section 5.1. Therefore, under the ‘look through’ approach, P’s total ownership interest in S is 72% (and the NCI’s interest is 28%). P’s effective ownership interest of 72% in S represents 60% held directly and an additional effective interest of 12% (40% × 30%) via A. Therefore, P consolidates the profits of S of CU1,000,000, with: Profits of S attributable to the owners of P **CU720,000 (CU1,000,000 × 72%)** Profits of S attributable to NCI **CU280,000 (CU1,000,000 × 28%)** However, when equity accounting for A, P does not recognise its share of S’s results recognised by A of CU120,000 (CU1,000,000 × (40% × 30%)). This is to avoid double counting as S’s results have already been consolidated and P’s effective ownership of 12% of S that is indirectly held via A has been attributed to the owners of the parent. The assets and liabilities of S are consolidated, with: Net assets of S attributable to the owners of P **CU2,160,000 (72% × CU3,000,000)** Net assets of S attributable to NCI **CU840,000 (28% × CU3,000,000)** Investment in A CU440,000 (Note 1) Overall impact on P’s consolidated net assets (including equity accounting) **CU3,440,000** • Of which, attributable to the owners of P **CU2,600,000 (CU3,440,000 - CU840,000)** Note 1 - when equity accounting for A, P does not recognise its share of S’s net assets recognised by A of CU360,000 (CU3,000,000 × (40% × 30%)) to avoid double counting as the net assets of S owned by A have already been included in the amounts attributable to the owners of P. Therefore, the investment in A is reported at P’s 40% share of A’s net assets (excluding A’s share of S), i.e., 40% × CU1,100,000. Approach 2 (‘black box approach’) Under this approach (the ‘black box approach’), when determining NCI, P considers the ownership interests in S not held by the group (i.e., by P and its subsidiaries). This approach views the equity method as primarily a method of valuing an investment, as further discussed in Chapter 12 at section 5.1. Accordingly, P’s ownership interest is 60% and the NCI’s ownership interest is 40%. Therefore, P consolidates the profits of S of CU1,000,000, with: Profits of S attributable to the owners of P **CU600,000 (60% × CU1,000,000)** Profits of S attributable to NCI **CU400,000 (40% × CU1,000,000)** The assets and liabilities of S are consolidated, with: Net assets of S attributable to the owners of P **CU1,800,000 (60% × CU3,000,000)** Net assets of S attributable to NCI **CU1,200,000 (40% × CU3,000,000)** Investment in A CU800,000 (Note 2) Overall impact on P’s consolidated net assets (including equity accounting) **CU3,800,000** • Of which, attributable to the owners of P **CU2,600,000 (CU3,800,000 - CU1,200,000)** Note 2 - when equity accounting for A, P does not eliminate its share of S’s results of CU120,000 and net assets of CU360,000. Therefore, the investment in A is reported at P’s 40% share of A’s net assets (including the associate’s share of S), i.e., 40% × (CU1,100,000 + (30% × CU3,000,000)). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests The results and net assets in respect of S (including the effect of equity accounting for A’s holding in S) that are attributed to the owners of P are the same as in Approach 1. However, the total consolidated results and net assets exceed those reported in Approach 1 by CU120,000 and CU360,000 respectively (being P’s effective ownership interest of 12% in S’s profits and net assets that are equity accounted in Approach 2). While this approach has the merit that the attribution of S’s profits and net assets to NCI reflects the 40% ownership interests in S not held by the group, a downside is the double counting of 12% of S’s profits and net assets that are equity accounted by A. 5.4 Measurement of NCI where a subsidiary owns shares in parent Shares of a parent held by a subsidiary are reflected as treasury shares rather than outstanding shares in the statement of changes in equity. The same applies for the purposes of computing earnings per share (see Chapter 33 at sections 3.2 and 4.1). This is shown in the following Illustration. Illustration 5-5: Presentation of shares in a parent held by a subsidiary in the parent’s consolidated financial statements Entity P owns 100% of subsidiary S. At the start of the period, S purchased 1 million shares in the parent for CU2m. In its individual financial statements, S records the investment at fair value through OCI which is recorded at CU3m at the end of the period. In preparing P’s consolidated financial statements, the following journals are required: CU m CU m Dr Treasury shares (parent equity) Dr Parent’s share of OCI Cr Investment in parent shares In computing earnings per share, the 1 million treasury shares are excluded from the number of shares included in the denominator. Where there is NCI (with a present ownership interest) in a subsidiary, the NCI has an interest in the net income of the parent by virtue of the shares of the parent that the subsidiary owns. Consequently, the subsidiary’s ownership share in the profit (or loss) and OCI of the parent would be included in determining the amounts attributable to the NCI in the subsidiary. Care, however, needs to be taken to avoid any double counting when determining the NCI. 5.5 Presentation and disclosure of NCI in primary financial statements IFRS 10 requires NCI to be presented in the consolidated statement of financial position within equity, separately from the equity of the owners of the parent. [IFRS 10.22]. This amount is the aggregate of all NCI if there is NCI in more than one subsidiary. The entity’s profit or loss and each component of OCI are attributed to the owners of the parent and to the NCI. Attribution of total comprehensive income to the NCI continues even if it results in a deficit balance. [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B94]. See section 2.2 above and section 5.7 below for further discussion. Deficit balances are considered further in section 5.7.1 below. The presentation of NCI in the primary financial statements is addressed in Chapter 3 at section 3.1.5 (statement of financial position), [IAS 1.54], section 3.2.2 (statement of profit or loss), [IAS 1.81B], section 3.2.4 (statement of comprehensive income), [IAS 1.81B], and section 3.3 (statement of changes in equity). [IAS 1.106]. There are similar requirements under IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial Statements.30 The allocation of profit or loss attributable to owners of the parent and NCI is presented in the statement of profit or loss outside the five specified categories of income and expense [IFRS 18.76, IFRS 18.104, IFRS 18.107]. See Chapter 4 at sections 3.4 and 3.4.3. IFRS 12 includes further disclosures in respect of subsidiaries that have NCI that are material to the reporting entity. [IFRS 12.10(a)(ii), IFRS 12.12]. See Chapter 14 at section 4.2. IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial Statements, which supersedes IAS 1 for reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027 (with earlier application permitted) was issued by the IASB in April 2024. See Chapter 4 for further details on the new concepts and requirements of IFRS 18. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 5.6 Non-controlling interests classified as financial liabilities In spite of the general requirement in IFRS 10 to treat non-controlling interests as equity, a non-controlling interest is classified by IAS 32 as a financial liability and payments to the non-controlling interest as interest expense if the group as a whole has an obligation to deliver cash or another financial asset in respect of the instrument, or to settle it in a manner that results in its classification as a financial liability. [IAS 32.AG29]. See Chapter 43 at section 4.8.1. The subsidiary may have issued certain puttable instruments and instruments that impose an obligation on the entity to deliver to another party a pro rata share of the net assets of the entity only on liquidation. While such instruments impose a contractual obligation on the entity, they may meet the conditions to be classified as equity instruments under paragraphs 16A-16D of IAS 32 in the separate or individual financial statements of the subsidiary. [IAS 32.16A, IAS 32.16B, IAS 32.16C, IAS 32.16D]. See Chapter 43 at section 4.6. This exception does not extend to the classification of non-controlling interests in the consolidated financial statements of the group, where such instruments are classified as liabilities. [IAS 32.AG29A]. This position was confirmed by the IFRS IC in November 2013.31 In some cases, the effect of options over what are by law non-controlling interests may be such that no NCI is recognised in the financial statements, particularly when such options are issued as part of a business combination (see section 6 below). How we see it In our view, where NCI is classified as equity in consolidated financial statements, it is subject to all the requirements of IAS 32 relating to own equity. For example, put or call options over NCI accounted for as equity should be accounted for in consolidated financial statements as contracts over own equity instruments under IAS 32 (see Chapter 43 at section 11). These requirements do not apply where the non-controlling interests are classified as financial liabilities, or the effect of options means that there is no NCI recognised in the financial statements. 5.7 Subsequent measurement of NCI Extract from IFRS 10 Non-controlling interests B94 An entity shall attribute the profit or loss and each component of other comprehensive income to the owners of the parent and to the non-controlling interests. The entity shall also attribute total comprehensive income to the owners of the parent and to the non-controlling interests even if this results in the non-controlling interests having a deficit balance. B95 If a subsidiary has outstanding cumulative preference shares that are classified as equity and are held by non-controlling interests, the entity shall compute its share of profit or loss after adjusting for the dividends on such shares, whether or not such dividends have been declared. A proportion of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity is only attributed to those instruments included within NCI if they give rise to an existing ownership interest. NCI that are potential voting rights and other derivatives that require exercise or conversion (such as options, warrants, or share-based payment transactions) generally do not receive an allocation of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B89, IFRS 10.B94]. However, as discussed in section 2.2 above, allocating the proportions of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity based on present legal ownership interests is not always appropriate. An entity also considers the eventual exercise of potential rights and other derivatives if, in substance, they provide an existing ownership interest that currently gives it access to the returns associated with that ownership interest. In that case, the proportion allocated to the parent and NCI takes into account the eventual exercise of those potential voting rights and other derivatives. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B90]. As noted in section 2.2 above, this chapter uses the term ‘present ownership interest’ to include existing legal ownership interests, together with potential voting rights and other derivatives that, in substance, already provide existing ownership interests in a subsidiary. IFRIC Update, November 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests If a subsidiary has outstanding cumulative preference shares classified as equity that are held by NCI, the parent is required to compute its share of profits or losses after adjusting for the dividends on these shares, whether or not such dividends have been declared. [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B95]. This effectively means that the NCI represented by the cumulative preference shares are being allocated a portion of the profit or loss equivalent to the dividends. This accounting treatment is shown in Illustration 5-6 below. Illustration 5-6: Attribution to NCI where subsidiary has outstanding cumulative preference shares classified as equity Entity X’s ordinary shares are owned by its founders, Entity A (which has a controlling 70% ownership interest) and Entity B (which has 30% ownership interest). X also issued CU400,000 preference shares to Entity C which entitle holders to receive a 5% cumulative preferred dividend. However, as the payment of the fixed rate dividend is at the issuer’s discretion and there is no maturity date or holder redemption right, the preference shares are classified as equity. On liquidation, the preference shares are entitled to repayment at par and cumulative unpaid dividends (whether or not declared). X is not a limited life entity. The profits (losses) of X are CU60,000 (in Year 1), CU(10,000) (in Year 2) and CU30,000 (in Year 3). Because C is entitled to a 5% cumulative dividend on CU400,000 shares, i.e., CU20,000 per year, the profit of X attributable to the NCI in A’s consolidated financial statements is as follows: Year 1 Year 2 Year 3 CU’000 CU’000 CU’000 Profit (loss) of X (10) Less amounts attributable to the NCI in preference shares held by C (1) Profit (loss) attributable to ordinary shareholders (30) Owners of parent (70%) (21) **NCI (30%) (2)** (9) Total NCI ((1)+(2)) (in ordinary shares and preference shares) Although X incurred a net loss in Year 2, the NCI attributable to the preference shares would be attributed income associated with its stated dividend for that year. The NCI presented in the statement of profit and loss and other comprehensive income and in the statement of changes in equity will be the sum of the NCI for the ordinary and preference shares, i.e., CU32,000, CU11,000 and CU23,000 in Years 1 to 3 respectively. In some cases, an entity may have a complex equity structure with several classes of equity shares or other contractual arrangements specifying different entitlements to net profits, equity or liquidation preferences. In such cases, the parent needs to assess carefully the rights attaching to each class of equity share or the effect of the contractual arrangements in determining whether and, if so, how these impact the appropriate attribution of profit or loss. The assessment will be very dependent on the particular facts and circumstances. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Illustration 5-7 below presents an example of a situation where the arrangements specify different contractual entitlements to net profits and final distribution of net assets to the proportionate ownership interests of the equity shares. Illustration 5-7: Allocation of NCI where contractual entitlements to net profits and net assets differ from the proportionate ownership interests Entity X and Entity Y set up Subsidiary Z, to undertake a real estate development for subsequent sale. The share capital of Z is owned 70% by X (which has been determined to control Z) and 30% by Y (which is the NCI) with X investing CU7 million and Y investing CU3 million. Under the shareholder’s agreement between X and Y: • No dividends will be distributed until the exit date, i.e., on disposal of the developed property, which is expected to occur in 3 years. • There is a contractual waterfall for the final distribution of Z’s net assets which will vary depending on the final profit achieved: • The first CU1 million of profit is allocated only to Y. • The next CU0.5 million of profit is allocated 50:50 to X and Y. • Any profit in excess of CU1.5 million is allocated 70:30 to X and Y. • Losses will be allocated 70:30 to X and Y, in accordance with their proportionate shareholdings. The asymmetric allocation of profits reflected the fact that Y, a passive investor, demanded a higher return for its funding, given the riskiness of the venture. The allocations are consistent with the entitlements of X and Y on liquidation of Z. At the end of the reporting period, the profit is CU0.9 million. However, the final cumulative profit in Year 3, based on the approved business plan, is forecast to be CU4 million. How should the NCI be determined for reporting periods prior to the exit date? How we see it IFRS 10 does not specifically address how to attribute profits to the NCI where the entitlements under the contractual arrangement differ to the proportionate ownership interests. Judgement is, therefore, required, based on the particular facts and circumstances. In our view, an appropriate method in this case could be determination of the NCI in line with its entitlement, based on the cumulative profit achieved at each reporting date, applying the contractual waterfall rules. Where material, it is important that the basis of allocation and the judgements involved are disclosed. The initial balance of the NCI is CU3 million. However, the profit from Z will not be allocated to the parent until there is a cumulative profit exceeding CU1 million. Accordingly, the NCI at the end of the reporting period is CU3.9 million (i.e., CU3m+CU0.9m). This is consistent with the fact that X, the parent, would not receive distribution of profit from Z if it was liquidated at the end of the reporting period. This conclusion could be analogised to IFRS 10.B95 which explains that if a subsidiary has outstanding cumulative preference shares that are classified as equity and are held by NCI, the entity computes its share of profit or loss after adjusting for the dividends on such shares, whether or not such profits have been declared. [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B95]. Where a gain or loss is recognised by a non-wholly-owned subsidiary in an upstream transaction with its parent, the gain or loss is eliminated on consolidation. Therefore, the profit or loss allocated to NCI excludes the NCI’s share of that eliminated gain or loss (see section 2.4.1 above). Where a subsidiary has granted options over its own shares under an equity-settled share-based payment transaction, the share-based payment expense recognised in profit or loss will be attributable to the parent and any other NCI that has a present ownership in the subsidiary. None of the expense is attributed to the NCI represented by the options under the share-based payment transaction. The corresponding entry taken to equity by the subsidiary in respect of the options under the share-based payment transaction will be recognised as NCI in the consolidated financial statements, as shown in Illustration 5-8 below. Illustration 5-8: Share-based payment over a subsidiary’s shares On 1.1.20X0, Entity X, a wholly-owned subsidiary with 900 shares in issue, grants share options over 200 shares to its employees with a grant-date fair value of CU300 which vest at the end of 2 years’ service. The aggregate exercise price (if all options were exercised) is CU50. At the end of 20X1, 50% of the employees exercise their options over 100 shares. Therefore, the parent P’s ownership interest in X falls to 90%. Prior to the exercise of the awards, the net assets of X are CU975. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests At the end of 20X2, the remaining share options lapse unexercised. In P’s consolidated financial statements, the following entries are reflected: CU CU 20X0 Dr Share-based payment expense (P&L) Cr NCI in share options 20X1 Dr Share-based payment expense (P&L) Cr NCI in share options Note that, as X is a wholly-owned subsidiary, the share-based payment expense is allocated to P as the holders of the share option awards do not have a present ownership in X’s shares. If the shares in X were instead partly owned (e.g., owned 80% by the parent), the share-based payment expense would be allocated to the parent (80%) and the NCI (20%) in accordance with their present ownership. However, the grant of the share option awards gives rise to an NCI in X’s equity, so NCI is increased by the full amount of the credit entry in the statement of changes in equity and equals CU300 at the end of 20X1 (immediately prior to the exercise of the share option awards). At the end of 20X1, 50% of the awards are exercised and X issues 100 shares to employees for CU25 cash. The employees now own 10% of X’s shares and X has net assets of CU1,000. The exercise of the awards is reflected as follows: CU CU Dr Cash Dr NCI in share options exercised (50% x CU300) Cr NCI in shares (10% x CU(975 + 25)) (‘ordinary NCI’) Cr Parent equity In 20X2, X makes profits of CU200 which are allocated between owners of the parent (90%) and ordinary NCI (10%). The remaining share options lapse unexercised and therefore the remaining NCI of 150 in the share options is derecognised against parent equity (e.g., retained earnings). Consequently, there only remains ordinary NCI in X’s shares. The ordinary NCI will be allocated a 10% share of X’s profit or loss and OCI. Closing NCI represents 10% of X’s net assets of CU1,200. These accounting entries would be reflected in the Statement of Changes in Equity as follows: Parent equity NCI Total equity CU CU CU At 1.1.20X0 Profit for year (150) (150) Share-based payment of subsidiary At 31.12.20X0 (150) - Profit for year (150) (150) Share-based payment of subsidiary Exercise of share options in subsidiary (50) At 31.12.20X1 (225) Profit for the year Lapse of share awards (150) - At 31.12.20X2 Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Ordinary share dividends paid to NCI proportionate to the NCI’s shareholding are changes in equity allocated to the NCI. Accordingly, an ordinary share dividend paid to NCI reduces cash (or other net assets) and the carrying amount of NCI in the consolidated financial statements. When the proportion of equity held by the NCI changes (and the parent retains control of the subsidiary), the carrying amount originally recognised in NCI is adjusted to reflect the change in the relative interests in the subsidiary. [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B96]. This situation might arise, for example, if a potential voting right like a share option is exercised into ordinary shares of the subsidiary carrying a proportionate right to dividends. Accordingly, the carrying amount of the NCI, if any, relating to the potential voting right now exercised is derecognised but, in its place, NCI representing the proportionate interest in the underlying shares now held is recognised. The difference between the consideration received by the parent on exercise of the option, and the adjustment to the carrying amount of NCI is recognised in parent equity. How we see it In our view, the requirements in IFRS 10 mean that if potential voting rights lapse unexercised, the amount originally recognised in NCI, if any, is reversed to parent equity, so that the carrying amounts of the controlling and NCI reflect the relative interests in the subsidiary. Otherwise, amounts previously recognised related to lapsed potential voting rights would remain recognised as part of the NCI until the next remeasurement of NCI occurs, which may be an unrelated transaction, or which may never occur. 5.7.1 Loss-making subsidiaries Total comprehensive income is attributed “to the owners of the parent and to the non-controlling interests even if this results in the non-controlling interests having a deficit balance.” [IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B94]. This approach is consistent with the fact that the controlling interest and the NCI participate proportionately in the risks and rewards of an investment in the subsidiary. The IASB observed that “although it is true that non-controlling interests have no further obligation to contribute assets to the entity, neither does the parent.” [IFRS 10.BCZ165]. Guarantees or other support arrangements by the parent often protect the NCI from losses of the subsidiary in excess of their equity. The IASB believes that the parent ought to account for such arrangements separately, and that the accounting for these arrangements should not affect how an entity should attribute comprehensive income to the parent and the NCI. [IFRS 10.BCZ162]. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Call and put options over NCI Some business combinations involve options over some or all of the outstanding shares. For example, the acquirer might have a call option, i.e., a right to acquire the outstanding shares at a future date for a particular price. Alternatively, the acquirer might have granted a put option to the other shareholders whereby they have the right to sell their shares to the acquirer at a future date for a particular price. In some cases, there may be a combination of such call and put options, the terms of which may be equivalent or may be different. How we see it IFRS 3 gives no guidance as to how to account for a combination of call and put options over NCI in a business combination. Therefore, when determining the appropriate accounting in the consolidated financial statements in such situations, IFRS 10, IAS 32, and IFRS 9 need to be considered. The accounting for call and put options depends on whether or not the acquirer has obtained present access to returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares of the acquiree subject to the call and/or put option. Potential voting rights that represent a present ownership interest are discussed in general in sections 2.2 and 5.7 above but specific considerations for call and/or put options are discussed in sections 6.1 and 6.2.2 below. While the discussion below deals with options, similar considerations to those discussed in sections 6.1 and 6.2 below apply where the acquirer entered into a forward purchase contract for the shares held by the other shareholders (see section 6.3 below). Although the discussion in sections 6.1 to 6.3 below focuses on call and put options entered into at the same time as control of the subsidiary is gained, an entity may enter into the options with non-controlling shareholders after gaining control. This situation is discussed in section 6.4 below, although the appropriate accounting in the consolidated financial statements will still be based on the discussions in sections 6.1 to 6.3 below. How we see it Sometimes, the operation of put and/or call options over NCI are subject to employment conditions. Typically, such put and/or call options over NCI arise as part of a business combination. The guidance in sections 6.1 to 6.5 below is intended to apply to put and/or call options over NCI held in the holder’s capacity as a shareholder. Accounting for put and/or call options over NCI subject to employment conditions (holders of such instruments are referred to below as ‘employee-shareholders’) involves a careful analysis of how the requirements of the relevant IFRS accounting standards apply to the specific transaction. Such arrangements could, in whole or in part, constitute an employee benefit (accounted under IAS 19 Employee Benefits - see Chapter 31 at sections 12 and 13) or share-based payment arrangement (accounted under IFRS 2 - see Chapter 30). Judgement is often required in determining whether and to what extent there is a remuneration element to be accounted for and whether the company or the NCI have a present ownership interest in the shares that are legally owned by the NCI. Examples of situations which might indicate a remuneration element include where: • There is a discount on the NCI put exercise price if the employee-shareholder leaves before completing a specified period of service • The entity can exercise a call at a lower exercise price than the exercise price on the NCI put if the employee-shareholder leaves In certain arrangements, an employee-shareholder is entitled to a minimum amount even if no service was provided and it might be appropriate to account for part of the arrangement in line with the guidance for NCI puts and calls (in sections 6.1 to 6.4 below), and part as an employee benefit or share-based payment arrangement. See Chapter 30 at sections 2.2 and 2.2.4, and Chapter 31 at section 2.2 for a discussion of when cash-settled awards may fall within the scope of IAS 19 or IFRS 2. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Even if the NCI put held by employee-shareholders has an exercise price equal to fair value of the shares at the date of exercise, this does not necessarily mean that the NCI has a present ownership interest in the shares legally owned at the date of the business combination. For example, it might be the case that the NCI are able to benefit from dividends and/or fair value gains accreting to the shares only after completion of a period of service. In such cases, it might be considered that the shares have not economically vested in the NCI at the date of the business combination. This assessment will be very dependent on the particular facts and circumstances. 6.1 Call options only Call options are considered when determining whether the entity has obtained control, as discussed in # Chapter 7 at section 4.3.4. Where it is determined that an entity has control over another entity, the proportions of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity allocated to the parent and NCI are based on present ownership interests, and generally do not reflect the possible exercise or conversion of potential voting rights under call options. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B89, IFRS 10.B94]. The eventual exercise of potential voting rights under the call option is reflected in the proportion of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity only if in substance the entity already has access to the returns associated with that ownership interest. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.24, IFRS 10.B90, IFRS 10.B94]. This assessment depends on the terms of the call option, and judgement is required. 6.1.1 Options giving the acquirer present access to returns associated with that ownership interest A call option is likely to give the acquirer present access to returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares subject to the call in limited circumstances, for example: • When the option price is fixed with an exercise price sufficiently low in relation to the fair value of the underlying shares (that substantially all expected variations in the fair value of the underlying shares will be realised by the acquirer) and either (i) it is agreed between the parties that no dividends will be paid to the other shareholders (or the acquiring entity can control that no dividends will be paid prior to the exercise of the option) or (ii) the dividend payments lead to an adjustment of the option exercise price Or • When the terms are set such that the other shareholders effectively receive only a lender’s return This is because any accretion in the fair value of the underlying ownership interest under the option (for example, due to improved financial performance of the acquiree subsequent to the granting of the call option) is likely to be realised by the acquirer. How we see it If a call option gives the acquiring entity present access to returns over all of the shares held by non- controlling shareholders (see discussion above), then there will be no NCI presented in equity. The acquirer accounts for the business combination as though it acquired a 100% interest. The acquirer also recognises a financial liability for the present value of the exercise price to be paid to the non-controlling shareholders under the call option. Changes in the carrying amount of the financial liability are recognised in profit or loss, in accordance with IFRS 9. If the call option expires unexercised, then the entity has effectively disposed of a partial interest in its subsidiary in return for the amount recognised as the ‘liability’ at the date of expiry. Where the entity continues to control the subsidiary, it accounts for the transaction as a change in ownership interest without a loss of control, as discussed in section 4 above. Where the lapse of the call option leads to a loss of control, this is accounted for as discussed in sections 3.2 to 3.5 above. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 6.1.2 Options not giving the acquirer present access to returns associated with that ownership interest A call option is not expected to give the acquirer present access to the returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares subject to the call where, for example, the option’s terms contain one or more of the following features: • The option exercise price has not yet been determined or will be the fair value (or an amount that approximates fair value) of the underlying shares at the date of exercise • The option exercise price is not sufficiently low in relation to the fair value of the underlying shares (that substantially all expected variations in the fair value of the underlying shares will be realised by the acquirer) • The option exercise price is based on expected future results or net assets of the subsidiary at the date of exercise • It has been agreed between the parties that, prior to the exercise of the option, all retained profits are to be freely distributed to the existing shareholders according to their current shareholdings (and there is no adjustment to the option exercise price for dividends paid) If a call option does not give the acquirer present access to the returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares subject to the call, IFRS 10 requires that the instruments containing the potential voting rights be accounted for in accordance with IFRS 9. [IFRS 10.21, IFRS 10.B91]. Derivatives on an interest in a subsidiary are accounted for as financial instruments unless the derivative meets the definition of an equity instrument of the entity in IAS 32. [IFRS 9.2.1(a)]. How we see it The accounting for the call option over NCI by the parent in its consolidated financial statements depends on whether the call option meets the definition of a financial asset or an equity instrument. Where settlement is not exclusively by an exchange of a fixed number of shares for a fixed amount of cash (or another financial asset), the purchased call option does not meet the definition of an equity instrument in IAS 32 and is treated as a derivative financial asset. [IAS 32.11(d), IAS 32.26, IAS 32.27, IFRS 9 Appendix A]. Therefore, the classification will be a financial asset, unless both the fixed-for-fixed criterion is met and the option is gross-settled in the subsidiary’s equity instruments. The classification of purchased call options is discussed further in Chapter 43 at sections 5, 5.2.8, 11 and 11.2.1. Financial asset - A purchased call option is initially recognised as a financial asset at its fair value, with any subsequent changes in its fair value recognised in profit or loss. If the call option is exercised, the fair value of the option at that date is included as part of the consideration paid for the acquisition of the NCI (see section 4 above). If it lapses unexercised, any carrying amount is expensed in profit or loss. Equity instrument - A purchased call option is accounted for in a similar way to a call option over an entity’s own equity shares, as discussed in Chapter 43 at section 11.2.1. This is because it is an option over the NCI in the consolidated financial statements, and IFRS 10 regards the NCI as ‘equity’ in those financial statements. Because such a call option over the NCI’s shares will be gross settled, the initial fair value of the option is recognised as a debit to equity (and is not subsequently remeasured). If the call option is exercised, this initial fair value is included as part of the consideration paid for the acquisition of the NCI (see section 4 above). If a call option lapses unexercised, there is no entry required within equity. The above discussion addresses the initial debit entry on recognising the call option at fair value (as a financial asset, or within equity). However, the related initial credit entry will depend on the transactions giving rise to the call option. For example, the entity may have paid consideration for the option in a separate transaction or as part of a larger transaction (such as where a business combination involves an outright purchase of shares as well as the acquisition of a call option over the remaining shares). In the latter case, part of the consideration paid in the larger transaction needs to be allocated to the acquisition of the call option. 6.2 Put options only Under current IFRS accounting standards, it is not clear how to account for put options that are granted to holders of NCI (‘NCI puts’) at the date of acquiring control of a subsidiary (or, indeed, after gaining control). There is a lack of explicit guidance in IFRS accounting standards and potential contradictions between the requirements of IFRS 10 (in respect of accounting for NCI and changes in ownership without loss of control - see sections 4 and 5 above) and IAS 32 (see section 6.2.1 below). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests How we see it The accounting for put options over NCI has been the subject of much debate over the years. It is clear that, under current IFRS accounting standards, the NCI put itself gives rise to a liability representing the present value of the exercise price (see section 6.2.1 below for a discussion of the measurement of this liability). However, there are a number of decisions that must be made in order to account for the arrangements, including: • Whether or not the terms of the NCI put mean that it gives the parent a present ownership interest in the underlying securities (see section 6.2.2 below) • Where the parent does not have a present ownership interest, whether or not an NCI continues to be recognised, i.e., does the parent recognise both the NCI and the financial liability for the NCI put (see section 6.2.3 below) In the latter case, there are a number of additional decisions that must be made, in particular the basis on which the NCI is recognised. Although the IFRS IC unequivocally confirmed as early as 2006 that an NCI put with an exercise price to be settled in cash is itself a financial liability,32 the nature of the financial liability remains controversial and has been discussed by the IFRS IC and the IASB on a number of occasions. The previous deliberations have been in the context of an NCI put that is required to be settled in cash. The IFRS IC has also considered a request in 2016 regarding how an entity accounts for an NCI put in its consolidated financial statements where the NCI put has a strike price that will be settled by delivery of a variable number of the parent’s own equity instruments. Specifically, the IFRS IC was asked to consider whether, in its consolidated financial statements, the parent recognises: (a) A financial liability representing the present value of the option’s strike price - in other words, a gross liability Or (b) A derivative financial liability presented on a net basis measured at fair value. The IFRS IC was also asked whether the parent applies the same accounting in its consolidated financial statements for NCI puts for which the parent has the choice to settle the exercise price either in cash or by way of delivery of a variable number of its own equity instruments to the same value. However, on the basis of its previous discussions and the IASB’s Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity (FICE) project (see section 7.3 below), the IFRS IC decided in November 2016 not to add this issue to its agenda.33 How we see it Given that the IFRS IC did not conclude on this matter, in our view, both approaches to accounting for an NCI put - for which the parent must or has the choice to settle the exercise price by way of delivery of a variable number of its own equity shares - are acceptable. Therefore, the parent is permitted to measure the financial liability at the present value of the option’s strike price or as a derivative financial liability measured at its fair value. In June 2018, the IASB issued a DP, Financial instruments with Characteristics of Equity34 which further considered the accounting for NCI puts and in November 2023 issued an Exposure Draft Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity - Proposed amendments to IAS 32, IFRS 7 and IAS 1.35 At the time of writing, the IASB is considering stakeholder feedback on the proposals, and is expected to issue final amendments during 2026.36 The Exposure Draft is discussed further in section 7.3 below. Like the previous deliberations of the IFRS IC and the IASB, the discussion that follows relates to NCI puts that are required to be settled in cash. IFRIC Update, November 2006, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, November 2016, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Discussion Paper DP/2018/1, Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity, IASB, June 2018. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Exposure Draft (IASB/ED/2023/5) Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity - Proposed amendments to IAS 32, IFRS 7 and IAS 1, IASB, November 2023. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. *Reference: IFRS Foundation work plan and IFRS - Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity project page (accessed 26 September* 2024), available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 6.2.1 The financial liability for the NCI put Extract from IAS 32 With the exception of the circumstances described in paragraphs 16A and 16B or paragraphs 16C and 16D, a contract that contains an obligation for an entity to purchase its own equity instruments for cash or another financial asset gives rise to a financial liability for the present value of the redemption amount (for example, for the present value of the forward repurchase price, option exercise price or other redemption amount). This is the case even if the contract itself is an equity instrument. One example is an entity’s obligation under a forward contract to purchase its own equity instruments for cash. The financial liability is recognised initially at the present value of the redemption amount, and is reclassified from equity. Subsequently, the financial liability is measured in accordance with IFRS 9. If the contract expires without delivery, the carrying amount of the financial liability is reclassified to equity. An entity’s contractual obligation to purchase its own equity instruments gives rise to a financial liability for the present value of the redemption amount even if the obligation to purchase is conditional on the counterparty exercising a right to redeem (eg a written put option that gives the counterparty the right to sell an entity’s own equity instruments to the entity for a fixed price). As indicated in section 5.1 above, IFRS 10 regards the NCI as ‘equity’ in the consolidated financial statements. Therefore, in applying IAS 32.23, any contractual obligation to purchase NCI - such as an NCI put - gives rise to a financial liability measured at the present value of the redemption amount which is subsequently measured in accordance with IFRS 9 (see Chapter 43 at section 5.3 and section 11.3.2). [IAS 32.23]. IAS 32 offers no guidance as to how the financial liability is measured if the number of shares to be purchased and/or the date of purchase are not known. How we see it In our view, in measuring the NCI put liability it would be consistent with the requirement of IFRS 13 that liabilities with a demand feature such as a demand bank deposit have to be measured at not less than the amount payable on demand, to adopt a ‘worst case’ approach (see Chapter 15 at section 10.5). In other words, it should be assumed that the purchase will take place on the earliest possible date for the maximum number of shares. This is also consistent with IAS 32’s emphasis, in the general discussion of the differences between liabilities and equity instruments, on a liability arising except to the extent that an entity has an ‘unconditional’ right to avoid delivering cash or other financial assets. See also # Chapter 43 at section 5.3. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Figure 6-1: Decision tree for accounting for NCI puts The diagram above (and explanations about the different approaches given in sections 6.2.3.A to 6.2.3.D below) indicate that, under Approaches 1, 2 and 4, changes in the IFRS 9 financial liability subsequent to initial recognition are recognised in profit or loss. This would be the case regardless of whether the financial liability is subsequently remeasured at amortised cost or at fair value through profit or loss under IFRS 9. However, if the financial liability is designated at fair value under IFRS 9, the fair value changes attributable to changes in own credit risk are normally recognised in OCI (see Chapter 46 at section 2.4). How we see it Under all approaches, a financial liability representing the present value of the exercise price is recognised for an NCI put settled in cash. The accounting for the remaining aspects of the NCI put depends in part upon an assessment of the terms of the transaction and, in some areas, involves a choice of accounting policy which, once selected, must be applied consistently. Figure 6-1 above summarises the analysis that we believe needs to be performed, the questions to be addressed and the approaches to be applied. 6.2.2 The NCI put provides a present ownership interest How we see it In our view, in the same way as for call options, an entity has to consider whether the terms of the transaction give it present access to the returns associated with the ownership interest in the shares subject to the NCI put. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests Factors to consider in determining whether the NCI put might provide a present ownership interest to the acquirer include: • Pricing - to the extent that the price is fixed or determinable, rather than being at fair value (or an amount that approximates fair value) of the underlying share - so that substantially all the variations in fair value of the underlying share accrete to the acquirer. However, in situations where there is only an NCI put, the level of the exercise price needs to be sufficiently high compared to the fair value of the underlying shares to exceed likely fair value variations in order to provide a present ownership interest. If the exercise price is low compared to fair value of the underlying shares, it is unlikely that the NCI will exercise the put. Even if the exercise price exceeds fair value of the underlying shares at the outset, reasonably possible upward fair value movements might mean that the NCI put does not provide a present ownership interest to the acquirer. • Voting rights and decision-making - to the extent that the voting rights or decision-making connected to the shares concerned are restricted. • Dividend rights - to the extent that the dividend rights attached to the shares concerned are restricted. It is likely that the NCI retains a present ownership interest where it has been agreed between the parties that, prior to the exercise of the option, all retained profits are to be freely distributed to the existing shareholders according to their current shareholdings (and there is no adjustment to the option exercise price for dividends paid). However, in many cases, dividend rights do not affect the assessment as the parent (through its control) can generally prevent distributions to the NCI prior to exercise or the exercise price of the NCI put is adjusted downwards for payment of any dividends to the NCI. • Issue of call options - a combination of NCI puts and calls, with the same period of exercise and same or similar pricing indicates that the arrangement is in the nature of a forward contract. This feature means that the instrument is highly likely to be exercised by one of the counterparties in situations where the exercise price is not at fair value (or an amount that approximates fair value) of the underlying shares. See section 6.3 below. The parent will generally have obtained a present ownership interest if there is an NCI put and call with matching terms and a fixed exercise price (or a fixed price forward contract to purchase the entity’s shares held by the NCI). Where the pricing of the NCI put and call is at fair value (or an amount that approximates fair value), the present ownership will generally remain with the NCI. Other situations, including where the exercise price is based on a formula, are likely to require further analysis, based on the factors outlined above. If it is concluded that the acquirer has a present ownership interest in the shares concerned, it is accounted for as an acquisition of those underlying shares, and no NCI is recognised. The accounting is described in section 6.2.3.A below. 6.2.3 The NCI put does not provide a present ownership interest How we see it When the terms of the transaction do not provide the parent with a present ownership interest of the shares subject to an NCI put, there are four approaches that can be taken. Key policy decisions that management must make in order to conclude an accounting approach, are: • Due to the potential contradictions between IAS 32 and IFRS 10, which standard takes precedence? • If an NCI is recognised on initial acquisition, whether or not it continues to be recognised? If the entity chooses to base its accounting policy on IAS 32, i.e., IAS 32 takes precedence, then it will only recognise a financial liability for the NCI put and not recognise an NCI. The approach is described in section 6.2.3.A below. If the accounting policy choice is that IFRS 10 takes precedence, the entity will initially recognise both the NCI and the financial liability under the NCI put. It initially measures any ‘qualifying NCI’ (i.e., those components of NCI that are both present ownership interests and entitle their holders to a proportionate share of the entity’s net assets in the event of a liquidation), either at fair value or at the proportionate share of net assets, with this choice available for each transaction (as discussed in section 5.2.1 above). [IFRS 3.19]. There is then a further accounting policy choice as to whether the NCI that was initially recognised continues to be recognised (as described in sections 6.2.3.B to 6.2.3.D below). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 6.2.3.A NCI is not recognised - financial liability recognised (Approach 1) Approach 1 must be used when the entity has a present ownership interest in the shares concerned (see section 6.2.2 above). Approach 1 could be used when the entity does not have a present ownership interest but concludes that IAS 32 takes precedence over IFRS 10. By recognising a liability for the put option over the shares held by the NCI, no NCI is recognised. The business combination is accounted for on the basis that the underlying shares subject to the NCI put have been acquired. Thus, if the acquirer has granted a put option over all of the remaining shares, the business combination is accounted for as if the acquirer has obtained a 100% interest in the acquiree. No NCI is recognised when the acquirer completes the purchase price allocation and determines the amount of goodwill to recognise. The consideration transferred for the business combination includes the present value of the amount payable upon exercise of the NCI put to the non-controlling shareholders. Approach 1 is based on the requirements and guidance within IAS 32. IAS 32 requires the NCI put to be recognised as a liability, as discussed in section 6.2.1 above. [IAS 32.23]. IAS 32 explains that when a subsidiary issues a financial instrument and a parent or other entity in the group agrees additional terms directly with the holders of the instrument (e.g., a guarantee), it could be the case that the group does not have discretion over distributions or redemption. Even though the subsidiary appropriately classifies the instrument without regard to these additional terms in its financial statements, their effect is taken into account in the consolidated financial statements. To the extent that there is such an obligation or settlement provision, the instrument (or the component of it that is subject to the obligation) is classified as a financial liability in the consolidated financial statements. [IAS 32.AG29]. The implication is that the underlying financial instruments (i.e., the shares in the subsidiary) are represented by the financial liability. Accordingly, since the shares held by those non-controlling shareholders are not treated as equity interests in the consolidated financial statements, there is no NCI to be accounted for under IFRS 10. This means that the profit or loss (and changes in OCI) with respect to the subsidiary are allocated to the owners of the parent and not to the NCI, as there is none. Under this approach, any dividends paid to the other shareholders are recognised as an expense in the consolidated financial statements, except where they represent a repayment of the liability (e.g., where the exercise price is adjusted by the dividends paid). The NCI put is accounted for as a financial liability under IFRS 9. [IAS 32.23, IFRS 9.4.2.1, IFRS 9.4.2.2]. Changes in the carrying amount of the financial liability are recognised in profit or loss. If the NCI put is exercised, the financial liability is extinguished by the payment of the exercise price. If the NCI put is not exercised, then the entity has effectively disposed of a partial interest in its subsidiary, without loss of control, in return for the amount recognised as the financial liability at the date of expiry. The entity accounts for the transaction as discussed in section 4 above. The consideration received is the amount of the financial liability extinguished and any difference between this and the carrying amount of the NCI (as of the date that the NCI put expires) is recognised within equity attributable to the owners of the parent (i.e., parent equity). 6.2.3.B Full recognition of NCI (Approach 2) Approach 2 is one of the alternatives that could be used when the entity does not have a present ownership interest in the shares concerned and concludes that IFRS 10 takes precedence. The acquirer initially recognises the NCI, either at fair value or at the proportionate share of the acquiree’s net assets. [IFRS 3.19]. Approach 2 takes the view that the NCI continues to be recognised within equity until the NCI put is exercised. The carrying amount of the NCI changes due to allocations of profit or loss, allocations of changes in OCI and dividends made for the reporting period (see section 5.7 above). The financial liability for the NCI put is recognised at the present value of the amount payable upon exercise of the NCI put and is subsequently accounted for under IFRS 9 like any other written put option on equity instruments. On initial recognition, the corresponding debit is made to another component of parent equity, not to the NCI. All subsequent changes in the carrying amount of the financial liability that result from the remeasurement of the present value of the amount payable upon exercise of the NCI put are recognised in the profit or loss attributable to the owners of the parent, and not the NCI’s share of the profit or loss of the subsidiary. If the NCI put is exercised, the entity accounts for an increase in its ownership interest as an equity transaction (see section 4 above). Consequently, the financial liability, as remeasured immediately before the transaction, is extinguished by payment of the exercise price and the NCI purchased is derecognised against parent equity. If the NCI put expires unexercised, the financial liability is reclassified to the same component of parent equity that was previously reduced (on initial recognition). Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 6.2.3.C Partial recognition of NCI (Approach 3) Approach 3 is one of the alternatives that could be used when the entity does not have a present ownership interest in the shares concerned but initially applies IFRS 10 and recognises the NCI, either at fair value or at the proportionate share of the acquiree’s net assets. [IFRS 3.19]. Under Approach 3, while the NCI put remains unexercised, the accounting at the end of each reporting period is as follows: (a) The entity determines the amount that would have been recognised for the NCI, including an update to reflect allocations of profit or loss, allocations of changes in OCI and dividends declared for the reporting period, as required by IFRS 10 (see section 5.7 above). (b) The entity derecognises the NCI as if it was acquired at that date. (c) The entity recognises a financial liability at the present value of the amount payable on exercise of the NCI put in accordance with IFRS 9. There is no separate accounting for the unwinding of the discount due to the passage of time. (d) The entity accounts for the difference between (b) and (c) as an equity transaction. If the NCI put is exercised, the same treatment is applied up to the date of exercise. The amount recognised as the financial liability at that date is extinguished by the payment of the exercise price. If the NCI put expires unexercised, the position is unwound so that the NCI is recognised at the amount it would have been, as if the put option had never been granted (i.e., measured initially at the date of the business combination, and remeasured for subsequent allocations of profit or loss, OCI and changes in equity attributable to the NCI). The financial liability is derecognised, with a corresponding credit to the same component of parent equity that was previously reduced. 6.2.3.D NCI is subsequently derecognised (Approach 4) Approach 4 could be used when the entity does not have a present ownership interest in the shares concerned and concludes that IFRS 10 takes precedence. When the NCI put is granted in a business combination, the acquirer initially recognises the NCI, either at fair value or at the proportionate share of the acquiree’s net assets. [IFRS 3.19]. When the parent recognises the financial liability for the NCI put, it derecognises the NCI. There are two ways of viewing this, but the accounting effect is the same: • This transaction is an immediate acquisition of the NCI. The NCI is treated as having been acquired when the NCI put is granted, as in Approach 1. However, in accordance with IFRS 10, any difference between the liability recognised (at the present value of the amount payable upon exercise of the NCI put) and the amount of NCI derecognised is recognised directly in equity. (Under Approach 1, the difference is reflected in the measurement of goodwill.) • This transaction is viewed as a reclassification of an equity instrument to a financial liability. In accordance with IAS 32, when the financial liability is recognised, the present value of the amount payable upon exercise of the NCI put is reclassified from equity with the effect that the NCI is derecognised. Any difference between the carrying value of NCI and the liability is adjusted against another component of parent equity. The financial liability for the NCI put is subsequently accounted for under IFRS 9, with all changes in the carrying amount recognised in profit or loss. Dividends paid to the other shareholders are recognised as an expense of the group, unless they represent a repayment of the liability (e.g., where the exercise price is adjusted by the dividends paid). This means that the profit or loss (and changes in OCI) with respect to the subsidiary are allocated to the parent and not to the NCI, as there is none. If the NCI put is exercised, the carrying amount of the financial liability at that date is extinguished by the payment of the exercise price. If the NCI put expires unexercised, the liability is derecognised with the NCI being reinstated as if nothing happened. Any difference between the liability and NCI is recognised against another component of equity, generally the same component reduced when the liability was initially recognised. Consolidation procedures and non-controlling interests 6.2.4 Assessing whether to account for multiple transactions as a single arrangement As discussed in section 3.4 above, IFRS 10 provides guidance on when to account for two or more arrangements as a single transaction when a parent loses control of a subsidiary. [IFRS 10.26, IFRS 10.B97]. However, neither IFRS 10 nor IFRS 3 specifically addresses the accounting for a sequence of transactions that begins with an acquirer gaining control over another entity, followed by acquiring additional ownership interests shortly thereafter.
